2130. WILLIAM FISHER was indicted for breaking and entering the warehouse of John Wright, at St. Martin's in the Fields, and stealing therein, 1 pipe, value 12 s.; and 2 hogsheads of wine, 80 l.; the goods of John Wright.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN WRIGHT . I am a wine-merchant, and live at Wright's Hotel, in the Strand. The prisoner was formerly my wine-cooper—he left my service last September, not of his own accord—I have a cellar in Hungerford-market—I had thirty-five pipes of wine there in Dec, but there were four pipes of port which had come in a day or two previously—they were placed close by the door—I saw them safe on the 18th of December, and missed one on the 23rd, which was placed close to the door—I had a person in my employ named Hubbard—I had the key of the vault in my possession—it was part of Hubbard's duty to go there—when I missed the pipe of wine, on the 23rd of Dec., the door was mutilated, as if with a crow-bar, or something of that kind—the door-posts and door were a good deal jagged—the lock was not taken off, but forced back—I first discovered it about twelve o'clock in the day—the value
of the pipe was 80 1/2.—the duty was all paid—I advertised for it—I have a crane there with which I raise my pipes of wine—it was my own wine.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When did you part with the prisoner? A. In September—the cellar is under the great hall of Hungerford-market—it is a floor between the market and the lower cellar—the floor of my cellar is on a level with the street—a friend went in, and saw the door in that state, and told me—I went with Hubbard—that is not in that part of the market where the trade is going on—it is in a private street, called Hungerford-street—there is a foot-pavement—there is a passage which leads to the cellar—the wine would have to be rolled from the cellar to the door of the cellar, and outside the door is the foot-pavement, but the crane is attached to the door—the wine would have to be raised by the crane, and put into the cart.
COURT. Q. Would a person have to roll the pipe? A. Yes, from the cellar to the street—it would require more than one person for the purpose of using the crane—it is easily rolled when put in motion—the crane is immediately over the cellar door—I should think it was from ten to twelve yards from the mouth of the cellar to where this wine was—my warehouse is in the parish of St. Martin in the Fields.
WILLIAM HUBBARD . I live in Baldwin's-place, and was clerk to Mr. Wright in Dec. last. I had the care of the cellar in Hungerford-market-—there were four pipes of wine there belonging to him, one lying nearly across the door—I saw them safe between four and five o'clock in the afternoon of the 20th of Dec.—when I left that day I secured the door—I went next on the 23rd with Mr. Wright and Mr. Tattersall, and found the cellar-door broken open—the lock had not been injured—I met the prisoner at the latter end of May, or the beginning of June, at the Hope Inn, Blackwall-street—I had never seen him before—I asked him how he was, and then if he had heard about the pipe of wine being stolen from the cellar—he laughed, and said it had never done him any good—I did not make any observation on it—I did not think that he had stolen it.
HENRY HOLMES . I am a corn-dealer, and live in Theobald's-road. I have known the prisoner as a wine-cooper—he applied to me one day in Dec. last to lend my horse and cart to move a pipe of wine—I am not certain that he told me where it was—it was in the afternoon, about three o'clock—it was light—a man named Goddin, who was in my service, then put the horse into the cart immediately, and it went away about half an hour afterwards—I can swear it went out before it was dark—I do not know what time it came home—he afterwards paid me half-a-crown.
ISAAC GODDIN . I live in Crown-court In Dec. last I was carman to Mr. Holmes—I had seen the prisoner a day or two before I went with him and Mr. Holmes's cart—I think it was in the week before Christmas—I cannot tell the day—I went some time in the evening—I think it was after five o'clock—we went to Hungerford-street, to those vaults which have been since pointed out to me by Mr. Wright—I have worked there one day since—I went with the prisoner to Hungerford-market—he told me to wait a bit, and he went away—I do not know where—he was absent about a quarter of an hour—he then came and told me to back the cart against the crane at this vault—there was nothing under the crane then—the prisoner then left me again—I did not see where he went—I did not look after him—when he came back a cask came out of the door against the crane, and two men craned it up into the cart—I had not seen those men with the prisoner, and I do not know what he was doing when they craned up the wine into the cart—the prisoner told me to go with it to the Dog and Duck yard—neither of us got into the cart—we walked—he was sometimes before the cart, and sometimes
behind—when we got to the Dog and Duck yard in Prince's-street, Red Lion-square, I drove into the yard—(it is about 400 yards from Theobald's-road)—the wine was then taken out of the cart, and I put the tail-board up and went home—I got half a pint of beer, or a pennyworth of gin, I am not sure which.
COURT. Q. Is the Dog and Duck yard inclosed? A. No, it is an open yard—ever so many belong to it—I left the prisoner there—I do not knot whether he stopped with the wine.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not there somebody else to receive the wine there? A. Yes, there was a brewer's man, or some wine-porter—he had a brewer's jacket—he and the prisoner assisted in taking the wine out of the cart—I got into the cart and left it with the brewer's man—I cannot give the day nor the time accurately, nor swear whether it was before or after tea that I took this cart—I think it was just getting dusk—when I got to Hungerford-street, I drew up opposite to a bottle-merchant's, about twenty yards from the cellar at first.
COURT. Q. What time did you get to Prince's-street, Red Lion-square? A. I do not know—I took no notice of the time—it took me about a quarter of an hour to go from my master's to Hungerford-market—I waited a quarter of an hour while the prisoner went away—he was absent a second time for about ten minutes—it was about an hour from the time I went out till I came back.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18440819-2131]
2131. JOHN NEWMAN was indicted for feloniously assaulting Catharine Lancaster, and taking from her person, and against her will, 1 purse, value 2 d.; 1 handkerchief, 3 d.; 14 shillings, and 3 sixpences; the property of George Lancaster, and heating, striking, and using other personal violence to her.
CATHERINE LANCASTER . I lived at Ealing, but have now removed to Hanwell—it does not matter whose wife I am—I live with Lancaster as his wife—I have been married—the name of my husband is Hawkins, I think—I live with the person I am with, and he keeps me—the policeman asked me, and I said I was the wife of George Lancaster—the man I married was named Hawkins, but we never cohabited—I was in service then—I was married in the parish church of Hammersmith, and was of full age—I do not know whether it was by banns—there was one person of the name of Guy there—I do not know the name of the other—I do not know whether I was married in 1840—I never saw the certificate—my father was a farmer—I do not know what Hawkins' father was—I have lived with George Lancaster three yean—I am usually known by his name—on Saturday night, the 3rd of Aug., he gave me 1 l.; 5 s., and on Monday, the 5th of Aug. I came to Hammersmith, to buy a cover for my table—I went to the Old Ship, where I waited, on account of the rain—I had two glasses of ale there—I took my purse out from my breast, and the prisoner saw me—when I went out, he followed me—I went down the Broadway 50 or 100 yards—I had before that taken my handkerchief out of my pocket, and put it over the purse in my breast—I heard a person come quickly behind me, and I was struck between the shoulders with a hand—I was knocked down—the prisoner put his hand in my bosom and took the purse and money and handkerchief out of my bosom—I saw him directly I got up, and I followed him—the policeman said to me, "I thought you were gone home"—he took the prisoner into custody—the man who knocked me down ran in the direction where the prisoner was taken—I lost a handkerchief, which was found on the prisoner—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PATNE. Q. Had you anything to drink at the Old Ship? A. Yes, two glasses of ale—I was there from between six and seven o'clock till about half-past ten—I was drinking with Knight and his wife—I bad no more than two glasses of ale—I had been in no other public-house that day—I just looked into the Hop Pole, where I had lived servant, to see my old mistress, but I might be in public-houses without drinking—I was not at the Cock and Magpie—Lancaster keeps me—Hawkins is a fool, and is in the workhouse—he was not there when he was married—my mistress turned me down stairs one Sunday morning, and my fellow-servants said, "You are not going to be married to such a fool?"—I said, "I am not"—they got me into the church, but I never bedded with him—he was no husband of mine.
Q. Do you mean that because your mistress told you, you went through the solemn service of marriage in Hammersmith church with a fool? A. If I bad known him I should not—I never spoke ten words to him in my life till that time—I am wholly supported by Lancaster—I have no children by him—I do not get part of my living by seeing other men—when Lancaster is out of work I make caps and gowns—I am not almost always tipsy—I have not been tipsy the last five days.
Q. Were you not going over the suspension bridge to drown yourself?
A. No—I went to get rid of company I did not like—I could go that way home to Ealing—it was not seven miles round—I went to get out of their company, and said I was going that way—when I paid the toll I did not take the halfpenny out of my bag—I had it in my glove—I saw the prisoner directly after I had been knocked down—he got away from me and nobody else—I had seen the prisoner come into the Old Ship, and he was standing at the door of the Old Ship when he was taken—I had not been dancing in the tap-room—I never danced in my life—I did not offer-to treat a man named Walpole with a cigar—I did not speak to anybody but Knight and his wife—I did not offer to treat the prisoner—Mrs. Knight came after me on to the bridge—she did not say she came to prevent me from drowning myself—I was not going to drown myself in a drunken fit—I do not want for anything—I will swear that when I paid the toll-keeper I had my money about me—I was not very much the worse for liquor when I paid—the toll-keeper did not allow the policeman and me to pass without paying the toll because I was in custody for having attempted to drown myself—I paid the toll to come back—the policeman came on the bridge and took me down the Broadway to get the purse—I swear I paid the toll twice—I paid for what I had to drink.
WILLIAM TRIGG ( police-constable T 96.) I was on duty in the Bridge-road, Hammersmith. At half-past nine o'clock that evening I saw the prisoner talking to a female at the door of the Old Ship public-house, and heard him say twice to the female, "Go and fetch her back"—the prosecutrix was then going towards the bridge, and the woman the prisoner had addressed went and spoke to the prosecutrix—I noticed that the prosecutrix was the worse for liquor, and I tried to induce her to come back to an omnibus—I then went round my beat till a quarter past ten, and noticed the prisoner running from the direction of the Broadway to the Ship public-house—the prosecutrix came up to me in the same direction that the prisoner had been running—she complained of having been robbed, and said she should know the man if she could see him—I took her over to the Ship—the prisoner was at the door, and the prosecutrix pointed him out—she said she had lost amongst other things a handkerchief—I found on the prisoner at the station two handkerchiefs, one of which she claimed—I have no doubt that the prisoner is the roan who was running, and the prosecutrix after him—there was no other
person ran in that direction or entered the public-house—there was a lamp over the door, and I could see him by that light—no one but my brother officer passed between the time of his running to the Ship and the prosecutrix coming up to me.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know her before? A. No—I did not understand that a drunken woman was going on the bridge to destroy herself, but I knew the party and heard the conversation—I went on the bridge and brought the prosecutrix back to the Broadway, and persuaded her to wait for the omnibus—I saw her again in about three-quarters of an hour—I did not pay any toll in going on or coming off the bridge—I do not know whether she paid—it was not more than three minutes after I saw the prisoner running to the public-house that I saw the prosecutrix—the prisoner was standing at the door of the public-house when I got up there—I had been standing about three yards from the Ship, opposite to it—the prosecutrix had been drinking, but she had her common senses about her—she said she had been robbed of her purse by a man who she should know again if she could see him—I knew the prisoner before—he did not bear a good character—he lived at the Ship about twelve months, and had left it aboot three months—I did not find the purse or money.
CATHARINE LANCASTER re-examined. This handkerchief is mine—I know it by the hemming—it is my work—I bought it out of Mr. Lancaster's money—I lost fourteen shillings and three sixpences of his money.
( Witnesses for the Defence.)
JOHN KNIGHT . I am a carpenter, and live in Cromwell's-buildings, Hammersmith. I know the prosecutrix by the name of Mrs. Hawkins, but not by the name of Lancaster—I saw her on the evening of the 5th of Aug., at the Ship, about half-past five, standing at the bar, and she had a quartern of gin—I was standing at the door—she asked me to go into the tap-room with her, and have a drop of beer—I went in with her, and had a drop of beer—she ordered six or seven pots, altogether—when she was pouring out the second glass of gin, the glass was broken—I think she stopped there till about ten at night—I was there all the time, drinking some of the beer which she had furnished—I and my wife went with her to the Angel, which is in the middle of Hammersmith town—we had a pot of beer there, and she had 3 d. worth of oysters—we came back to the Ship again, and the prisoner was in the tap-room—the prosecutrix told me to order a pot of beer—she then went out, and came back in about two minutes, and drank part of the beer—the prisoner was there then.
JURY. Q. Who paid for this beer and liquor? A. She did, except for the last pot—there were seven or eight people in the tap-room—she went round and treated them all.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you see what money she had? A. She emptied it out of a leathern bag into her left hand, in the beginning of the evening—there was a half-crown, two shillings, a sixpence, and some halfpence—it was about half-past ten o'clock when she came back to the Ship—I went with her into the town, and she had the oysters—we left the prisoner in the Ship—he had not been taken then—a person came to the door, and said a woman was gone over the bridge to drown herself—I and Cook went after her—when we got over the first pier, I saw the prosecutrix, the policeman, and my mistress—the policeman and prosecutrix came over the bridge together—I and my mistress left them—we went to the Ship again, and the prisoner was there—I saw a handkerchief something like this one, chucking about there—I believe it was this—it was blue, and had white diamonds on it—it
was thrown up against the prisoner, when he was standing by the fire to dry his feet—he had a pipe in his mouth—I threw the handkerchief at him—I remember the policeman eoming and taking the prisoner at the door—he had not been out of the tap-room from the time that I went in, after I had come off the bridge, till the officer took him, except to go to the door—I have known him fourteen or fifteen years—he has an honest character—I know Hawkins—I saw him yesterday in the Bridge-road.
COURT. Q. What induced the prosecutrix to give you and seven or eight other people beer? A. She was dancing with the prisoner in the tap-room—she paid for six or seven pots—she did not pay for the last—I saw her pay for all but that—she paid for the last pot with 4 d. in halfpence—I did not see her come up and complain that she had been robbed—I came to the door, and saw the officer collar the prisoner—the prosecutrix was standing at the back of the officer, saying the prisoner was the man that robbed her.
JURY. Q. Was she sober enough to distinguish between two persons? A. No—she paid the toll for the bridge.
JANE KNIGHT . I am the wife of John Knight. I was in company with the prosecutrix, Mrs. Hawkins, that night.
COURT. Q. Have you always called her so? A. Yes—I knew her before she was married—I was present when she was married at Hammersmith church—Hawkins is a bricklayer's labourer—I do not know that he is weak in his intellect—he works at Notting-hill—I saw him last night—Hawkins and she lived together—I have been to their house when they lived in Angel-lane, three or four years ago—she had lived at the Hop Pole when she was married—they were living as man and wife in Angel-lane, and she was servant at the Hop Pole.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Occasionally they were living at the lodging? A. Yes, and she lived at the Hop Pole—she did something there, and must not go there—she then lived with Hawkins, and after that she left her husband—I saw her about six o'clock in the evening on the 5th of Aug.—she asked if I would have something to drink—I hardly knew her, but she explained who she was—we went and had some—I did not know she had been drinking till she got into the house—she called for a quartern of gin, and thought she had halfpence enough for it, but she had not—she pulled out a leathern bag from her bosom and took out a shilling, and in so doing she broke the glass—she had then two shillings, a half-crown-piece, a sixpence and twopence, after she had paid for the glass—we went into the tap-room—several other persons came in—the prisoner came in, and began to dry his stockings—my husband asked where he had been—he said, "Looking for work"—he asked my husband if he would give him some tobacco—he said he had none to give him—the prosecutrix ordered a cigar for him, and paid for it—she was after that dancing in the tap-room with several men, one man named Cook—we remained at the Old Ship till nearly nine o'clock; then the prosecutrix, my husband, and I went to the Angel—we there had a pot of beer and threepenny worth of oysters—we left the prisoner and others at the Old Ship—we went back to the Old Ship—she ordered my husband to call for a pot of beer—she then went out, and the landlady said, "There is a woman gone on the bridge, follow her"—I went out after her—the toll-keeper told me to go up to her—I went up and said, "Mrs. Hawkins, what are you going to do?"—she said, "When I get home Lancaster will kill me"—as soon as I got to her the policeman came up, and we went off the bridge—my husband had come up while we were there—the policeman and the prosecutrix went off the bridge straight up the Bridge-road, towards the Broadway—I and my husband went back to the Ship—the prisoner was in the tap-room when we got there—the policeman afterwards came
and took him—he had not gone out of the tap-room from the time we went back till the policeman came—when the prosecutrix gave the prisoner in charge, I said, "Mrs. Hawkins, you are not going to say anything against him?"—she said, "Yes, I am; I shall be killed when I get home"—I saw a handkerchief kicking about the tap-room before we went to the Angel—it was a blue handkerchief, and like this one.
COURT. Q. What makes you say the prisoner did not go out? A. I am sure he did not—we went straight in to him at the Ship, and as we were coming out to go home, the policeman and the prosecutrix ran across—the policeman said, "Is this the man"—she said, "Yes," and he collared him—my husband and I had not been in the tap-room—when we went back, we stood in front of the bar—the landlady was cross because the last pot of beer was not paid for—the prisoner was in the tap-room—the door was open, and we could see through—there were several men there.
JURY. Q. Are you intimate with Hawkins? A. He has worked with my husband—the Ship is near the suspension-bridge—there is another public-house between them, called the Duke of Sussex—when we left the prosecutrix she went with the policeman towards the Broadway, and we went to the Ship.
COURT. Q. Is the Ship in the Broadway? A. No; the Ship is on the left-hand side; it lies towards the water, just as you go to the toll-gate.
WILLIAM TRIGG re-examined. The prisoner ran by me about 100 yards from the Ship, and the prosecutrix came up to me about a dozen yards from the Ship.
HENRY WALE . I am the toll-collector of Hammersmith-bridge. I know the prosecutrix. On the evening of the 5th of Aug. she came to the bridge, put her right hand in her bosom, pulled out a purse, or a bag, and paid me with a halfpenny—she was drunk—she did not speak to me—Mrs. Knight came running after her, and asked if there was a young person gone on the bridge—I said yes, and allowed her to go after her—the policeman came, and Knight, and Cook—when they went off the bridge they did not pay any toll, as I thought the policeman had got the prosecutrix in charge—she was in front of him—I saw her go on to the Broadway, which may be about 200 yards from the toll-gate—the Ship may be about 40 yards from the Broadway—about a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes afterwards, Mrs. Knight came and told me Newman was taken for robbing the prosecutrix, who went on the bridge—I knew Newman, and always knew him to be an honest person—he belonged to the Ship formerly—I cannot say how long he has left—he lived at Mr. Chapman's before he went to the Ship.
COURT. Q. In conducting the prosecutrix from the bridge to the Broadway, would the policeman have to pass the Ship? A. Yes, within ten or fifteen yards—near enough for a person standing in the door-way to see her.
JOSEPH WALPOLE . I lived as waiter at the Ship at this time—I had been there about seven weeks—I saw Mrs. Hawkins come into the house that night—(I never knew her as Mrs. Lancaster)—she came in between five and six o'clock, and was very drunk—the prisoner came in about a quarter of an hour afterwards, and he asked Knight for a bit of tobacco—the prosecutrix said, "Don't have tobacco, have a cigar"—she paid me 1 d. for one, and I fetched it for him—the prosecutrix was dancing about—she went on her knees, and then danced about again—she went out about half-past eight, and came in again about a quarter to nine—I did not know any of these parties before I went to live at the Ship—I remember some man came in and said, "There is a woman going to drown herself"—the prisoner had not gone out of the house from that time till he was taken, to my knowledge—he was
drying his stockings when he came in—he had got his stockings on and went out at the door to go home, and the policeman took him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440819-2132]
2132. JOHN RILEY was indicted for stealing 38 gallons of brandy, value 7 l.; 12 s.; the goods of the East and West India Dock Company, in a port of entry and discharge.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM OWEN BIGGS . I am foreman of the vaults of the East and West India Docks Company, at the Limehouse entrance. I was at my post, on Monday, the 5th of Aug., at a quarter past six o'clock in the morning—the prisoner is a lighterman—I saw him coming out of the dock in his lug-boat, the Orton, No. 774—my gate was the last he would have to pass before he got into the river—he ought to have produced a pass before he passed me—I asked him for one—he said he had not got one—his boat appeared empty—I said he must get a pass, or he could not go out—he came on shore, and went to get a pass—he would have to go to Anderson, the officer, at the south gate, for it—when he had gone I went into his boat, and, on lifting up the head-sheet boards, I found a number of bladders of brandy stowed away between the timbers of the boat, and a basket containing a gimlet, a cock, and centre-bit—he did not return to the boat—there is no business done in the dock on Sunday—I attend tide every day—I had seen the prisoner in the dock on Saturday evening, about a quarter to seven o'clock—that was past the time when, according to the regulation, any boat can go in or out—he had not his boat with him then—I had seen him on the Monday morning, before he came with his lug-boat—he was waiting till I opened the inner gates—he could not, according to the regulations, have had an opportunity of getting in at that early hour—my place opens at six—the Dock Company officers took possession of the brandy—Anderson came up, and assisted in searching the boat.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When you saw him on Saturday evening, did you see his boat? A. No—he ought to have gone to the right to get out of the dock, but he went to the left, towards the Honduras, which was on the north side of the north quay—she was not the only vessel that laid there—I do not know whether this brandy has paid duty—I was not put into prison for smuggling—I have been in the Dock Company's service seventeen years, and have risen to what I am from a labourer—I was in prison falsely for a very short time—they found that out afterwards—it was not for smuggling—it had nothing to do with brandy—I am not aware that I ever tasted brandy which had not paid the duty, I might have done it.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What were you charged with? A. I cannot tell—it is twenty years ago—I was admitted to bail.
JAMES ANDERSON . I am watchman to the East and West India Dock Company. On Monday morning, the 5th of Aug., a few minutes before six o'clock, I saw the prisoner on the bridge leading over the lock to go to the Export Dock, and then I saw him in his lug-boat coming out of the Export Dock into the basin—I was at the gate—he asked me for a pass—I told him I would attend to him directly—he passed on to the outer lock, so that he got no pass—he went on towards the water-gate—he came back in a few minutes, and said, "I am come for my pass"—he was on land, without his boat—I said, "Come with me, and get it," it is my duty to examine the boat before I give the pass—I was going towards the water-gate for that purpose—he was with me—he turned his eyes towards the water-gate when he was on the import bridge—I looked the same way, and saw Biggs in the prisoner's
lug-boat—I do not know whether the prisoner saw him, but when I looked round again, he was out of sight—he had slipped away by some means—I proceeded to the water-gate, examined the boat, and saw the bladden, as described.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it not the usual practice to examine the boats, whether empty or full? A. Yes—we do not let any empty boats go out without—that is well known.
JAMES HESELTINE . I am an officer of the Docks. On the morning that the Orton was stopped I went on board her about seven o'clock, and found 43 bladders of brandy, measuring about 38 gallons and 1 quart—there were 13 gallons of dark brandy and 25 gallons and 1 quart of pale—I went on board the Honduras, which was in the Dock—her cargo was brandy—I sounded some casks—one was "N. B. 957," and one other after—I found they were not full—three of the largest casks had been pierced with a centrebit, and the centre-bit was found in the prisoner's boat—it would have made the holes—I tried it to the holes, and they fitted exactly—the holes were filled up with a cork—I have samples of each of the brandies here.
CHARLES COPPOCK . I am an officer of the Customs, and am searcher at the West India Docks. On the 5th of Aug. I went on board the Honduras, and found part of her cargo was brandy—I examined all the brandy there, and found 5 casks deficient—one of them was marked "N. B., 957," and was deficient 9 gallons, and there were 2 gallons deficient from one marked "D. G." with an "O." under it—there were 35 gallons deficient in the whole, some pale and some brown—I have tested the brandy that was in the casks and that in the bladders, and believe them to be the same.
WILLIAM DAVIS . I am night-watchman in the Dock. I was in the Dock on the night of the 3rd of Aug.—the Honduras was lying there—I saw the prisoner crossing the dock—I asked what he was doing—he said looking after his craft—it was after the men had knocked off work—I saw him there again at half-past seven in the evening.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see any craft near the Honduras? A. There were several there lying alongside—I saw three or four.
RICHARD SUMMERS . I am one of the coopers of the London Docks. I examine all casks before they leave the London Docks on the Brandy Quay—I examined all the brandies that passed from the London Docks on the 3rd of Aug., and on the 31st of July, I did not observe any tap-holes in any of them.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know how many left the Docks on that day? A. No—I do not know on what day this brandy was put on board the Honduras.
SAMPSON CAPPS . I am a dipping-officer. When these brandies were in the St. Katherine's Dock I re-dipped them all, and delivered six casks—I have got the marks here—"D. G." with "O." under, from No. 1 to 6—they were what we call "quarter casks"—they were all right in point of quantity when I examined them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see them put on board the Honduras? A. No.
NATHANIEL STEVENS . I am a re-dipping officer of the London Docks. On the 9th of July I received an order to deliver some casks of brandy—four of them were marked "N. D." in a diamond, from 956 to 959 inclusive, which was the export mark—they were from the Duren, and sent to the Honduras —I re-dipped them at the time of delivery—the No. "957 N. G." contained 56 gallons, and those four with the "S." contained 57 gallons.
JOHN BEALBY . I am foreman at the East Quay, at the London Docks. On
the 9th of July I delivered some casks marked "N. D." in a diamond, from 956 to 959, to Lucy's craft—on the 1st of Aug. I delivered two casks of brandy "S. 2 and 4," into Saye's craft—the name of the vessel was not mentioned—the contents marked are on the casks, but they are re-dipped, and there is a difference sometimes.
ROBERT GOODING . I am in the employ of Mr. Charles Lucy, a lighterman. On the 3rd of Aug. I delivered from his boat six casks of brandy, four marked "N. D." in a diamond, and two were "S."—there were no spill or auger holes in them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go on board the Honduras? A. Yes—I cannot say how many men were on board—there might be six or nine on deck, and three or four men below.
GUILTY .* Aged 29.— Transported for Ten Years.
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2133. MARY ANN SMITH and CAROLINE WILLIAMS were indicted for stealing 35 handkerchiefs, value 14 s., the goods of Elizabeth Knox; and that Williams had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES SWANN . I deal in toys, and live in Wellington-street, Waterloo-town. I occasionally keep a stall in Whitechapel-road—I had one there on Saturday, the 27th of July—about four in the afternoon I saw the prisoners together, walking up the street—they went to Mrs. Knox's shop—Williams took a bundle of handkerchiefs from the door, and gave them to Smith, who put them in her apron, and they walked away together—I saw Bleachmore, told him, and he followed them—I after that saw Smith in charge—I am sure they are the persons.
JOSEPH BLEACHMORE . I am servant to Elizabeth Knox, a hosier, in High-street, Whitechapel. Between four and five o'clock that day I left the shop on an errand—Miss Pearce, an aunt of my mistress's, was in the parlour—I was away a very few minutes—Swan saw me, and told me something—I looked on the stand at the door, and missed the bundle of handkerchiefs, which I had placed on a stand eighteen inches inside the door—I had seen them safe as I was going out—I followed the prisoners—they were then three doors off—Smith turned round to me and said, "What are you looking at?"—they turned round Essex-street—I saw Smith go to where they are pulling down a house, and she there let the bundle of handkerchiefs fall from her apron—I took them up—she then crossed over, and the two prisoners walked on together—the officer was sitting on the ruins—I ran to him, and gave Smith into custody, but not Williams, because I did not see her do anything—-these are my mistress's handkerchiefs.
CHARLES BYRON ( police-constable H 157.) From what Bleachmore said, I took Smith—Williams was apprehended about an hour and a half after I left—I heard Williams say that she was not in company with Smith, nor had she seen her that day.
Smith. I am quite innocent, and Williams was not in my company.
Williams. I know nothing about them.
MICHAEL CONWAY ( police-constable H 138.) I produce a certificate of Williams's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confineed Six Months.
WILLIAMS — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.

2134. HENRY JACOBS was indicted for stealing 2 saws, value 7 s. 6 d., the goods of Alfred Cohen; also 1 saw, 2 s., the goods of Robert Pantland, and that he had been before convicted of felony.
FREDERICK BOCK . I am a journeyman cooper, in the employ of Mr. George Blackman. I know the prisoner by sight—he lives in Gower-walk—about half-past six o'clock in the morning of the 22nd of July, I was piling staves in the yard—I saw the prisoner go across, and stoop down by the side of a beer-cooler that was in the road—he put his arm under and drew out three saws, placed them under his coat, and walked sharply away—I and Phillips followed—he got to Leman-street—I hailed him, and he came back—I asked him whose saws they were—he said his own—I asked where he was going to take them to—he said home, and that he had found them—I said, "They belong to some of Mr. Collier's men, I believe"—I said "I saw you take them from under the cooler"—I called a policeman, and gave him in charge.
WILLIAM PHILLIPS . I am a labourer. I was coming out of Mr. Blackman's yard—I saw the prisoner stoop down, put his arm under the cooler, draw out these three saws, and walk away with them—I went after him with Bock—I called him, and he came back—my mate asked him what he was going to do with them; but whether he said he was going to take them or not, I cannot say—my mate said he thought they belonged to some of Mr. Collier's men—he said, "If you think so, take them there and see"—he said he was going by and saw the handles of the saws sticking out, and picked them up.
WILLIAM HOWE ( police-constable H 168.) About half-past six o'clock I saw the prisoner and the two witnesses in Leman-street—Bock told me he saw the prisoner take up the three saws from under the cooler in Rupert-street—the prisoner said he found them, that he saw some chickens picking about there, which made him look under, and he was going to take them to Mr. Collier, perhaps they might belong to them—he gave his name Henry Jacobs, No. 2, Gower-walk—that is his name and address.
ALFRED COHEN . I am a pencil-maker. I have a workshop in Gower-walk—I know all these saws—two of them belong to me, and one to a person named Pantland, who was employed on the premises—on the 22nd of July I was at work—I had fastened the gate up the night before—I do not recollect where the saws were—I know they were there on the Saturday afternoon as late as four o'clock, under the work-bench—on Monday morning they were gone—I afterwards heard that they were taken to the station.
JOHN PANTLAND . I am a bricklayer. I have been employed the last three or four weeks in setting the steam-boiler at Mr. Cohen's—I went on Monday, the 22nd, and found the string which fastened the gate had been cut—this is my uncle's saw—his name is Robert Pantland.
DAVID HATTERSLEY ( police-constable H 90.) On Tuesday after this happened I heard something about this—I knew where the prisoner lived, and took him on the 3rd of Aug. in Church-lane, Whitechapel—I said, "You are the person who found these three saws?"—he said, "No, you are mistaken"—I took him to the station, and fetched Howe to identify him—the prisoner said it was not him, it was his brother—Howe fetched Phillips, and he still denied it—when asked by the sergeant on duty why he denied it, he said he had been in trouble before.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going to work and saw some chickens; I looked and saw these three saws; I took them up, and the witnesses came and asked where I got them.
CORNELIUS FOAY ( police-constable H 98.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18440819-2135]
2135. GEORGE EDWARDS was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Gilbart, about ten o'clock in the night of the 11th of August, at St. Marylebone, with intent to steal; and. that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. O'BRIEN conducted the Prosecution.
MICAIAH READ ( police-constable E 30.) At a quarter past eleven o'clock, on the night of the 11th of August, I was on duty on the prosecutor's premises, sitting behind a screen in the shop, with some coats hanging over it—I saw the prisoner and another man come to the back of the folding-doors—the other had a light—he tried to open the doors with keys for a quarter of an hour—he broke them open, and they came in—the prisoner said, "It is all right"—they came past me, and went to the door that leads to Oxford-street—his companion turned, and saw me—I got up, and seized his companion—the prisoner seized me by—, and pinched, and struck me—I let go of the other, and seized the prisoner—the other then went back to the other door, and was going out—the prisoner called out, "Come back, there is only one of them"—I felt something drawn across my hand, and found afterwards that my little finger was cut—I got to the door in Oxford-street, got a policeman, and took the prisoner—we found on him a bundle of skeleton-keys, and two of them open the door in Turk's Head-yard, which leads to the back of the premises—I had examined the doors at nine o'clock, when I went there, and they were all right.
JOSEPH SMITH ( police-constable E 136.) I was on duty, and was called by Read—I took the prisoner to the station—I can prove that the door in Turk's Head-yard was quite safe at ten o'clock.
WILLIAM GILBART . I am owner of the house No. 86, Oxford-street, in the parish of St. Marylebone—a daughter of mine resides on the premises, and two lodgers—it is my own house—the lodgens pay rent to me.
JOHN SIMS HANCOCK ( police-sergeant E 12.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY .— Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18440819-2136]
2136. MICHAEL FLEMING was indicted for feloniously cutting and wounding Bridget Fleming, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE YEOMAN HEATH . I am surgeon at University College Hospital. Between one and two o'clock in the morning of the 20th of Aug. I saw Bridget Fleming at the hospital, with a wound behind her left ear, about three-fourths of an inch in length—it did not reach the bone, but appeared as though some instrument had been pushed under the skin—I thought that a common dinner-knife, which was shown to me, was the most likely instrument to have effected it—I have seen her since, and some bruises appeared near the wound, which would make it less likely than I at first supposed—the wound was somewhat of a crescent shape, and there was a slight wound on the margin of the ear, which seemed a continuation of the other wound, as if the ear had been pressed against the head at the time the wound wasinflicted—the wounds were not dangerous—I only saw her once afterwards.
FREDERICK HARDEN ( police-constable E 88.) Bridget Fleming came to me about one o'clock that morning, in Grafton-street, and I took her to the hospital—I went with her to No. 2, Tottenham-street, and took the prisoner in bed in the kitchen—Bridget Fleming gave me the knife, which I produce,
and said, "This is what he has done it with"—I held the knife up to the prisoner, and asked him if that was his—he said it was—I could not see any other knife in the room.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was he very drunk? A. He appeared to have been drinking, but was perfectly sensible—he refused to go to the station, and said he should not get out of bed, he was very comfortable—he found his clothes, and dressed himself very well—I saw Bridget Fleming about half an hour ago.
( BRIDGET FLEMING was called, but did not appear.)
DAVID QUARRY ( police-constable E 94.) I assisted to take the prisoner to the station—he walked perfectly quiet—he might have been drinking, but was not drunk—he wished to know what he was going to be charged with—I said he would hear at the station.
JOHN MILLBANK . I am a coffee-house keeper, at No. 2, Tottenham-place, Tottenham Court-road. I heard a cry of "Murder" proceeding from the kitchen where the prisoner lives, about half-past twelve o'clock, that night—I had before that heard the prisoner and his wife having a few words—I heard cries more than once, but I did not take notice, as they were often quarrelling—I saw the street door open, and shut it—I saw the woman come with the officer, and the prisoner was taken to the station.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18440819-2137]

2138. CHARLES NORMAN and JOHN BULLEN were indicted for stealing 1 purse, value 6 d.; and 2 shillings; the goods of Jane Ann Stevens, from her person.
JANE ANN STEVENS . I am single. About a quarter-past ten o'clock in the evening, on the 8th of Aug., I was at an exhibition of wax-work in Holborn—I felt a hand in my pocket, where I had a purse and two shillings—I turned, and saw Norman had the purse in his hand—he dropped it on the floor—Bullen was close to him—this is my purse—it was safe in my pocket two minutes before—when Norman had it he was putting his hand across to Bullen.
CORNELIUS TAYLOR . I am check-taker at the exhibition. On the 8th of Aug. I saw the prosecutrix, about a quarter-past ten o'clock—as the man was going round describing the exhibition, the prisoners came in—they went directly to the prosecutrix—Norman made his way in front of her, and Bullen kept behind her—I saw Bullen put his hand into her pocket—he then got in
front of her—she said, "You have got my purse"—he said, "I have not, neither did I see it"—I saw him put his hand behind him, and hand it to Norman, who dropped it.
Bullen. I know nothing of it; I was looking at the wax-work, and they took me.

2139. JOSEPH LOVELL, JOSEPH BULL, GEORGE WILLIAMS , and JAMES PHIPPS , were indicted for stealing 2 sacks, value 2 s.; 4 bushels of oats, 13 s.; and 5 bushels of beans, 5 s.; the goods of George Stansby.
LAWRENCE ALEXANDER ( police-constable D 35.) At half-past five o'clock in the morning of the 4th of Aug. I received information, and went to Mr. Stansby's wharf, at Paddington—I found his stable door open and the inner loft door had been broken open—I went to a boat in the canal, and saw Williams and Phipps unloading some gravel out of it—I asked how, long they had been there—they said that they had been there all night, that they came at eight o'clock in the evening, and worked till half-past ten, then went to the Red Lion, came back at twelve, and worked till two, then laid down on a truss till daylight—I said, "I suppose you have heard about Mr. Stansby's place being broken, and the corn stolen?"—they said, yes they had—I asked if they had seen anybody—they said, "No"—I asked them if it was possible for any one to come there while they were at work, and they not see them—they said, no, it was not, and they had not seen any one—I went into the boat, and on the top of the deck I saw some loose corn scattered about—I lifted up the scuttle, and observed Bull and Lovell there—I asked what they did there—Lovell said he came to assist in unloading the boat; but that they had slept too long—I told them to get out—Lovell got out, but Bull seemed rather reluctant—I said, "Get out"—I saw something under him, and said, "What is that?"—he said it was nothing—I said it was something—he then said it was corn for the horses—I saw it was a sack—I then saw on the opposite side, where Lovell had been, some split beans—I asked who they belonged to—he said they were for the boat horses—I awoke the captain of the boat, who was asleep aft, and asked if he had got any corn—he said, "Yes, from three to four bushels" and he had two empty sacks, and a piece of a sack and a towing line—I asked if he knew about any beans—he said no, he never gave his horses any split beans, and if there were any they did not belong to him—I found about a bushel of beans, and a sack containing black oats, beside the sack of white oats I had found before—I sent for assistance to the station, and then the captain pointed out three bushels of black oats, which belonged to him—he said the white oats were not his—I had seen the prisoners pass me a few minutes before twelve o'clock—they were all together, and walking along the Wharf-road—when I found the place broken I traced beans from the stable to the boat.
JOHN HARVEY WHITELOCK . I live on board this boat—I had two empty sacks on board, and a piece of a sack—I had about three bushels of black oats on the deck of my boat, but no beans at all—I had the care of the boat—Williams and Phipps came to work about half-past eight o'clock on the Saturday night, to unload, and when I was going home, Phipps asked me to leave the deck unlocked that he might have a nap if it came on rain—I left it—I returned to the boat about twelve, and saw Williams and Phipps at work—I went straightway to bed, and know nothing more about it—when the policeman awoke me, I saw Lovell and Bull sitting on the beans—I did
not know that they were on the boat—I saw the sack of white oats in one of my sacks that I had had empty on board.
WILLIAM KENNEY . I am horse-keeper to Mr. George Stansby—he is a wharfinger, and lives at No. 9, Wharf-road. On Saturday night, the 3rd of Aug., at eleven o'clock, I left the stable all locked up safely—I went next morning, about five, and found the doors undone, and some beans scattered about—I went up to the loft, and found the inner door broken, and missed a sack of white oats, and some split-beans, which were my masters—the stable door was shut to when I found it, and it must have been opened from the inside—there is a wooden rack in the stable, and a person could get from there into the loft—I went with the officer to the boat, and saw Phipps and Williams—I saw some beans scattered from the stable to the boat—I compared the white oats, and the split-beans with those which were in my master's loft—they corresponded exactly.
Lovells Defence. I was at the Red Lion with Bull—Phipps and Williams came; we asked where we could lie down; they said they had got leave to lie down on the deck, and we might lie there, which we did, and the corn was there.
Williams' Defence. I saw the sack there when we came back from supper.
Phipps'Defence. When we left work there was no corn; when we came back it was there—these men asked us if we could tell them where they could lie down, and we did.
LOVELL — GUILTY . Aged 20.
BULL — GUILTY . Aged 23.
WILLIAMS — GUILTY . Aged 18.
PHIPPS — GUILTY . Aged 25.
Confined Six Months
[trialAccount t18440819-2140]
2140. JAMES DRISCOLL was indicted for stealing 1 breast-pin, value 1 s.; and 1 pair of gloves, 1 s. 3 d.; the goods of Edward Barker, from his person.
HANNAH MORRIS . I live in White Horse-street, Stepney. On the afternoon of the 9th of Aug., I saw the prisoner leading the prosecutor, who was rather intoxicated, down White Horse-street—the prisoner beckoned to a female as he came up to our door—I followed them till I met a policeman—the prisoner asked the gentleman to go up Bear-street—he said it was not the way home—the prisoner said he would take him round the other way—they went a little further, and the prisoner leaned upon his breast, against the wall in Butcher-row, and he took a pin from his breast—I had noticed the pin before—they both fell down—the prisoner was rather intoxicated—they went up Bear-street—I followed them, and they went down a kind of court or alley—I went and asked the gentleman to come out of the place—the prisoner said he would fetch a policeman, and give the gentleman in charge for half-a-crown, for bringing him from Blackwall-stairs—I begged of him to fetch one—he went and fetched one—we came down the Commercial road, down to Arbour-square—the gentleman, the prisoner, and I went to the station of our own accord, without a policeman—in going to the station the prisoner tapped me on the shoulder, and said to mind that he was halves—I saw the policeman, and gave the prisoner in charge—I saw some gloves and some silver taken from the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You had the drunken gentleman between you, the prisoner hold of one arm, and you the other? A. Yes—I have never been here as a witness before, nor in any court.
SIMON WILSON ( police-constable K 230.) I remember Morris coming to the station—I went out and met them—the prosecutor had hold of the prisoner
by the collar—he said, "I give this man in charge for robbing me of a breast-pin, a pair of gloves, and some money"—I took him to the station, and found this pair of gloves in his waistcoat pocket, and some money—directly the gentleman saw these gloves, he said, "These are mine"—Morris said, "Search him closely, you will find the pin"—he said, "No, that is all I have belonging to the gentleman"—I did not find any pin.
MARY ANN BEAZIER . I am the daughter of Mr. Barker, the gentleman that was robbed, and live in Lucas-street, Commercial-road. I saw him go out that morning—he had a gold pin—I know these gloves—I purchased them—he had them when he went out—he was going to a ship when he left in the morning.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose he got drunk? A. I cannot say—I saw him when he came home in the evening—he was not then so much in liquor but that he was able to command himself—he is now at home.
ANN WHITE . I am Mr. Edward Barker's sister. When he went out that morning I put the pin in his breast, and I gave him these gloves—he came home in the evening—he had no pin nor gloves then.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18440819-2141]

2142. WILLIAM RUSSELL was indicted for feloniously uttering a forged request for the delivery of goods, with intent to defraud James Howsden.
HANNAH HOWSDEN . I am the wife of James Howsden, brushmaker, in Holles-street, Marylebone. About two months ago the prisoner came to our shop—he said he was come for a set of blacking-brushes for Mr. Leslie (who is Lord Harewood's brother or son, and lives opposite to us)—I said I thought he had made a mistake, as we did not serve the family, and he went away—on the 8th of Aug. he came again, and said he had brought an order from the valet of Mr. Leslie for a set of blacking-brushes, and a clothes-brush—I let him have them—he said the valet would call the next day and pay for them—he mentioned about his having come before for brushes, and said, "This time I have brought a written order"—he said Mr. Hobbs, Mr. Leslie's valet, had given him the order.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Are you sure he said Mr. Hobbs had sent him? A. Yes—I did not know Mr. Leslie's family, but I knew Lord Harewood's servants, and I thought it might have come from them—( order
read)—"Mr. Howsden, brushmaker, Holles-street—Please to give the bearer one set of blacking-brushes, and one clothes-brush, for Mr. Leslie:—Camden-hall." ( The signature was unintelligible.)
THOMAS WEST ( police-constable C 58) On Thursday night, the 8th of Aug., the prisoner was given into my custody, charged with an attempt to get a pound of isinglass, under false pretences—he was dressed in a blue coat, and a striped livery waistcoat, breeches and gaiters—I took him to the station, and he told me he got the brushes from Robert Dukes, in May Fair—I found these four brushes on him, with the name of Howsden on them.
Cross-examined. Q. How soon afterwards did you apprehend him? A. About half an hour after he had been to Mr. Howsden's—I know where Mr. Lascelles lives—the prisoner was not near there.
MRS. HOWSDEN. These are the brushes I gave him.
WILLIAM HOBBS . I am butler and valet to Mr. William Leslie, of Camden-hall—I do not know the prisoner—I never sent him for these brushes—I never gave him any paper at all—this paper is not my writing—I never saw him till I was at the office.
Cross-examined. Q. How many servants has Mr. Leslie? A. Sixteen or seventeen—I questioned them all, and they said they did not know him—I asked the prisoner if he had any recollection of me—he said, "No."
GUILTY . Aged 55.— Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18440819-2143]

2144. ELLEN MURPHY was indicted for stealing 1 pair of trowsers, value 10 s.; 1 waistcoat, 7 s.; and 3 handkerchiefs, 3 s.; the goods of Luke Gibney.
LUKE GIBNEY . I lodge in Marylebone-lane, the prisoner lodged at the same place. This day fortnight, early in the morning, I went to work—I was back again about ten o'clock, and saw my trowsers, waistcoat, and three handkerchiefs safe—I missed them on the Wednesday afternoon—I had not seen them in the interval—the prisoner came to me and brought a constable with her—the constable asked what I had lost, and to describe the things to him, which I did—he said the prisoner had acknowledged to him that
she had pawned things of that description—I went to the pawnbroker's with her and found the things.
HENRY HALLIER . I live with Mr. Gray, a pawnbroker, in London-wall. I produce a pair of trowsers, a waistcoat, and three handkerchiefs, pawned by the prisoner on the 14th of Aug., in the name of Ann Davis, No. 3, Winchester-street—she said Mrs. Prescott had given her them to pledge.
JAMES EUSTON ( policeman.) The prisoner was given into my charge—she said another person gave them her to pledge—I believe she said it was Mrs. Prescott, but I will not be certain.
CAROLINE PRESCOTT . I lodge in Marylebone-lane. I met the prisoner at a friend's house—she was perfectly destitute, and I allowed her to stay at my place till she could get a situation—she was with me three weeks, and left last Friday morning—I did not give her these things—I was out the evening they were taken—I told her the preceding evening that she must leave my place, as she had stolen a pair of boots from a widow.
BENJAMIN BRYAN SHIRLEY . I am a carpenter, and lodge in Marylebone-lane. On last Tuesday week I came home in the evening between seven and eight o'clock—the prisoner came up hastily and knocked at the door, went up stairs, and opened the room door with the key belonging to Mrs. Prescott—she went back to Mrs. Prescott's room, and went up stairs—in a few minutes she came down with a bundle in her hand, came into my room, returned the key, and she said, "Don't mention to Mrs. Prescott that I have been here."
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. Mrs. Prescott employed me as servant, and sent me to pledge these things among others, one thing was a silver spoon, for which she was taken into custody.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Nine Months.

2147. JAMES BRIGDEN was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Ann Jones.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
ANN WILSON . I am the wife of Thomas Wilson, who is in the employ of the St. Katherine Dock Company—I knew the deceased, Ann Jones. On the 9th of July I was walking with her between King John's Palace and the Mulberry Tree, Stepney-green—there was a child in our way—we were walking in the kennel—there is no regular pavement there—Jones was on my right—it was very dusty—I thought I heard a distant sound of a wheel—I turned my head half way, when I just saw the side of the wheel—I was in the
act of putting up my hand to draw Mrs. Jones on one side when she was dashed down with violence before my feet—I looked up to see what had done it, and quite close to her saw the wheel—it was the left hand wheel—I cannot say that I saw the prisoner—I saw the reins, but in whose hand I cannot say—the deceased lay motionless—I raised her up—a man ran up to my assistance, and placed her head on his knees—I untied her bonnet string, and heard a gurgling kind of sound in her throat—I saw a large white contusion on her left temple—the blood poured profusely from her nostrils, and her left ear—there was a kind of paving-stone near where she fell, which forms the curb—there was no other vehicle in the road—the cart was on the left side of the way.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How wide is the footpath? A. Very wide indeed—the cart overtook us—Jones was on my left side at first—we had changed sides about five or six minutes before this happened—she stopped to pull up the heel of her shoe, or pick up a pin—that did not take her an instant—I suppose we might have got five or six steps after that when this happened, not more—she had then come to my right side—the wheel did not catch her clothes, it struck her on the shoulder—I saw it distinctly—the left wheel struck her violently on the shoulder, and struck her on the ground, and her left temple coming in contact with the stone produced the white mark I saw—we had no particular reason for walking in the kennel instead of the footpath—it was between three and four o'clock in the afternoon—no one else was walking the same way—the child was alongside of me—it was not above three years old.
MR. HORRY. Q. Were you on the footpath before you were in the road? A. No, we were in the kennel all the way.
THOMAS GIBLETT . I am a carrier, and live at Frome, in Somersetshire. On the 9th of July, between three and four o'clock, I was in the Stepney-road, near Stepney-green, with Allen—I was going towards King John's Palace, and saw the prisoner in a cart coming from it, driving at a very fast rate, I should say ten or twelve miles an hour—we passed two women—I cannot say whether Wilson was one of them—the prisoner passed me, and I had passed the women—after he had passed me I saw him in the middle of the road, as if thrown from the cart—I then turned, and saw the deceased lying with her head on the curb, about five or six yards from the prisoner—the cart was in the middle of the road—I went up to the deceased, took her up, and placed her head between my knees—blood was flowing from her nose—she did not move, but died almost directly—she was put into the cart, and I got into it—the prisoner was drunk—he drove part of the way to the hospital—he went at a good smart trot—I begged him not to drive so fast, for if there was life left in the woman, he would destroy it—another man got up, and I got out.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the cart far? A. No—I could not say whether the prisoner had been driving all the journey—I had been walking on the footpath—it is a wide one—there are gravel-stones on the footpath—perhaps the road is smoother—after I got out of the cart, I ran after it as fast as I could to the hospital—I was determined not to leave the woman—the road was rather dusty, but not so as to obscure the view of persons—there was a calf in the cart.
WILLIAM SELF . I am a surgeon, and live in Lucas-place, Commercial-road. I was on the spot, and saw the wheel come in contact with Mrs. Jones—I saw her soon after she was thrown down—I found her insensible, beginning to change colour, blood issuing from her nose and ear—I imagined that she had fractured her skull, and that turned out to be the case—I was at the post mortem examination.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe your opinion, having seen the cart coming, is, that it was coming at a smart pace? A. Yes—I was behind it—the prisoner did not fall till the collision had taken place—his fall was partly from the collision, and partly from attempting to stop the horse, immediately the accident had occurred.
COURT. Q. At what pace do you think it was going when it struck the woman? A. Ten or twelve miles an hour, I should think—the prisoner was intoxicated, in my opinion.
WILLIAM SMITH ( police-constable. K 220.) I took the prisoner into custody on the 9th of July, about four o'clock—he was drunk—he said it was not his fault, it was theirs; he was pulling up his horse, and fell out of the cart.
COURT. Q. How long did he appear under the influence of intoxication? A. I cannot say—I left him after half an hour or three quarters, and he was still drunk.
MR. CLARKSON to ANN WILSON. Q. Did you not say that when the deceased sprang to your side, and the child took the curb, she had hardly taken a few steps before you saw the man pulling the reins taut? A. No, I never saw the reins in the man's hand at all—( looking at her deposition)—this is my name—( reading) "I saw the man pulling the reins taut, and I think that turned the cart on her"—it seems my name is to this, but I did not see the reins in the man's hand.
(The prisoner received an excellent character.)
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Nine Months.

2159. DANIEL MULLIDAY was indicted for feloniously cutting and wounding John Beale, upon his head, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.—2nd COUNT, to resist his lawful apprehension and detainer.
MR. O'BRIEN conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN BEALE . I live at Deptford, On Thursday evening, the 20th of June, I saw the prisoner in Giffin-street, Deptford, creating a disturbance—I saw William Fuggle, the policeman, advise him several times to go in-doors—he said he would, and Fuggle left him, but finding he would not, he turned back to him, and said if he did not, he should certainly take him into custody—the prisoner then struck him a violent blow in the chest, and nearly knocked him backwards—he then ran into the passage of the house—Fuggle pursued after him, and in trying to take him a struggle took place in the place—he got Fuggle's head in between the door and door-post—I stepped up and asked him if I could render him any assistance—he said he should be obliged to me if I would, which I did, and by forcing the door back the constable released his head from his hat—the prisoner then ran up stairs into the back-room, first-floor—Fuggle requested me to go up with him, which I did—he asked the prisoner several times to open the door—he said he should not—at last he opened it, and struck me a violent blow across the head with an iron weapon of some kind—I said, "I am done"—I then fell back stunned and senseless—I was taken to Mr. Arthur's, who attended me for a month—I was not able to do anything for six weeks—I had done nothing to provoke the prisoner more than releasing the constable's head from the door.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did you know the prisoner? A. No—it is rather a noisy street, and celebrated for its disturbances—I was going home from my work—there were a great many men and women in the
street—the prisoner was saying he would fight any man or woman—there was no one making a disturbance but him—he was making use of very improper language—I did not get into the crowd—the prisoner had been drinking, but was sober enough to know what he was about—he appeared angry—after striking the policeman he ran past him into the house.
WILLIAM FUGGLE ( police-constable R 43.) On Thursday night, the 20th of June, I saw the prisoner in Giffin-street creating a disturbance, and abusing the persons that were by—he was offering to fight with nearly every one—when I came up I advised him to go home—he went away as if to go home—I had got about fifty yards from the place, when I was called back again, and found him still creating a disturbance—I again advised him to go home—he turned as if to go, but began again—I advised him a third time to go home, and followed him a little way down the street—finding he still persisted, I said if he did not go home I must take him to the station—with that he up with his fist, and struck me in the chest, which nearly took away my breath—he then ran away into the passage of a house—I pursued him—a scuffle ensued, and he got my head jammed in between the door and door-post—Beale was standing by, and asked if he could render me any assistance in taking the man into custody—I said I should feel obliged to him—he came to my aid, and we got the door open so as to extricate me—the prisoner redoubled his force against the door, and jammed my hat all to pieces—he then escaped up stairs and into a back room—I followed, and asked him several times to open the door—he refused—Beale was with me—he stood nearest the door—the very moment he opened the door he up with the poker, and hit Beale over the head with it—I distinctly saw the poker—Beale partly staggered back, and said, "Policeman, I am done, I am struck, lead me away to a doctor's"—a lad on the staircase took him away to a doctor's—the prisoner said, "If there are any more attempts to come into my room, I will murder them"—sergeant Wilton afterwards came, and we took the prisoner into costody—he was then lying on the bed.
JOSEPH ARTHUR . I am a surgeon, and live at Deptford. The prosecutor was brought to me on the night of the 20th of June last, between ten and eleven o'clock—he appeared stunned, and was bleeding very profusely from the head—he was not insensible—the scalp was completely divided at the anterior portion—it was a lacerated wound and severe, about an inch and a half long, and to the bone—the bleeding might have bad a beneficial tendency—he was under my care about a month—he was unable to leave his room for something more than a week—the symptoms were favourable—it must have been a very severe blow.
GUILTY on the 2nd Count. Aged 33.— Confined Six Months.

2163. JOHN WRIGHT was indicted for stealing 3 saws, value 11 s.; 3 planes, 6 s.; 1 stock and 3 bits, 4 s.; 4 guages, 2 s.; 1 spoke-shave, 1 s.; 2 gimblets, 6 d.; 6 chisels, 4 s.; 2 bradawls, 4 d.; 5 files, 1 s.; 2 gouges, 1 s.; and 1 pair of compasses, 2 d.; the goods of John Hawk.
JOHN HAWK . I am a carpenter, and live at Plumstead. On the 8th of July I was at work at a house in Beresford-street, Woolwich—I left work about twelve o'clock, leaving some of my tools in a cupboard, and some about the room—I returned about one o'clock, and saw the prisoner in custody at the back yard gate with the best of my tools in a basket by his side—here are three saws and the other articles stated—they are all mine.
JAMBS COLE . I am a wheelwright—I live in Beresford-street, opposite the house at which Hawk was at work. At a quarter-past twelve o'clock that day I was at dinner—I saw the prisoner try the doors and windows of the house, and the last window that he tried he went in at—I informed the policeman, and he was taken.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not say I was not the man? A. I said I was not certain of you—I said the man had corderoy trowsers, and something under his arm, but I turned the prisoner's coat back, and saw his blue sleeves, which I had noticed at first—he had not his coat on when he went in he had it under his right arm, and I saw his blue sleeves.
CHARLES LEVER ( police-constable R 72.) I received information from Cole, and watched the premises for about ten minutes—I then saw the prisoner come out at the back gate, and look about—he saw me, and placed himself against the gate post—I asked what he wanted there—he said he had been to ease himself—these tools were in a basket at his feet—he was out of the house, and coming into a back street—I asked where he brought the tools from—he said he knew nothing about them—I found no one there but him—about five minutes before he came out I had found the back gate bolted inside, so that no person could have gone in.
Prisoner. Q. How far was the basket from me? A. Close at your feet, and you was leaning against the gate-post—I left you in charge of a man while I searched the premises—it was impossible for you to enter at the back, you must have gone in the front, gone through the house, and opened the back door.
Prisoner's Defence, Cole said it was not me; they looked, and could find no one else, and then he said I was the man; anybody might have put these things up, and gone away.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18440819-2164]
2164. ELIZABETH DUBOIS was indicted for stealing 71 1/4 yards of lace, value 12 l.; 19 s., the goods of Francis William Vant.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS DAVIS . I am shopman to Francis William Vant, linen-draper, of Woolwich. On the 6th of Aug., about six o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came, and asked for some long cloth—I served her with some—she then asked for some thread edging—I served her with some, and with three-quarters of a yard of wider lace—while I was serving her I saw her draw two wide pieces off the counter, and put them under her cape—there were ten yards in one piece, and thirteen yards in the other—I made out her bill—she paid me, and went away—I followed her fifty or sixty yards from the shop, watched her all the time, and never lost sight of her—I then said to her, "I
have missed some pieces of lace, will you come back?"—she said, "O yes certainly"—she came back with me—I brought her to the spot where I had served her, and felt the lace-cards in her pocket—I then put her in the back parlour, and left her there by herself—I set the shop boy to watch—I went for a constable, and he took her.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you standing when you saw her draw the two pieces off the counter? A. Opposite her—I was looking at her—she took them in my presence off the counter, and put them under her cape—if I had seized her at the moment she would have made some excuse, so I thought I had better let her get out—I did not say on the first examination that I did not see her take any lace, but I thought some was gone, from the pieces being deficient—there were seven or eight other persons in the shop beside me and the prisoner—I wanted to search her, and she objected to it—the value of the two pieces 1 saw her take was 4 l.; or 5 l.;
JAMES HOLLOWAY . On the 6th of Aug. I saw the prisoner, and was set to watch her—I saw her take eight cards of lace from ber pocket, and place them under the drugget—I opened the parlour door, and said, "What made you put the lace there?"—she said, "Good God, young man, don't say I put the lace there; how cruel it will be if you do."
Cross-examined. Q. Where were you standing? A. At the parlour door—there is a window—it has a curtain to it, but it was one side—the prisoner was in the parlour—she could see me.
JOHN GRIFFIN ( police-constable R 229.) I took the prisoner on the 6th of Aug., and have the cards of lace—I got them from Davis—I asked the prisoner her name—she would not give it, nor say where she lived—she said I had no right to ask her.
JOHN DAVIS re-examined. This is my master's lace, and is worth 12 l.; 19 s. 2 d.—here are the marks on the cards.
GUILTY .* Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18440819-2165]
2165. WILLIAM HOMEWOOD was indicted for stealing 5 pair of horse-shoes, value 5 s.; the goods of James Moorey, his master; and JOHN NORRIS , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. CROUCH conducted the Prosecution.
JAMBS MOOREY . I am a veterinary surgeon, and live at Deptford. I have been in business since May last, and have lived in this house about three weeks—I succeeded my brother-in-law, Frederick Inglis, in the business, and Home wood was in his service—I went to the shop of Norris, who is a smith, in Church-street, Greenwich, and saw him, and saw my shoes hanging up—I went out of his shop, and wished him good morning—he locked his door and walked out with me—I saw a person, and told him the circumstances—I left Norris in the street, and about ten minutes after I returned, and told Norris that he had got some of my property—he said, "I have none"—I took them down, looked at about five pairs, and said, "These are all mine; but here is one small pair that I have made within a day or two; you will go with me to Homewood, and I will hear what he says about this"—he went with me to Homewood, who lived on my premises—when I got on my premises I called Homewood out, and showed him the shoes—I said, "You have been robbing me"—he said, "I have not"—I said, "You have robbed me, or made away with my property"—he said, "I have neither robbed you, nor made away with your property"—I said, "I shall give you into custody"—he said, "Don't be spiteful, I will tell you the whole truth; I have lent the horse-shoes to Norris, and that is all"—Norris said nothing, but I had heard Homewood say to
Norris, "Say you borrowed them"—I marked the horse-show, and detained them—the prisoners went away—I said, "I shall find, perhaps, some more"—I had discharged Homewood on the 6th of July—this was on the 11th—he was not in my service then, but was living on the premises still—he had had a week's notice to quit—I had him apprehended in the course of the same afternoon—I never authorized Homewood to lend or give away my shoes—these are them—this is a pair of my own making, which I took from Norris's shop—they are pony shoes—I had not sold them—no one sells in the shop but me.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Homewood had been in your brother-in-law's service two or three years? A. Yes, I believe he was respected by all that employed me—he was about taking a shop and forge of his own, of a man named Burrows—I went to Burrows—I did not persuade him not to let Homewood have the shop, nor tell him he would not have the rent—I did not say, "I will give you the same rent that he will, and will pay you now" nor that I could afford to keep it shut up, and he could not—I did not ultimately take it—I believe Homewood did—I did not say, if Homewood's wife had not been so insolent to me, I would not have brought the charge—I did not say so at any time to my knowledge—Mrs. Homewood wished the charge brought—this is my name to my deposition—it was read over to me—( looking at it)—I have said, that if the prisoner Homewood's wife had not been insulting to me, I should not have preferred this charge—I cannot recollect that—I have sworn to the best of my knowledge to-day—I do not know which is true—Mrs. Homewood came to me, and first begged me not to do it, and then she met me in the street, and said, "Now we will see it out if you like"—she has been several times to me—I did not say to Norris's wife, "You can send the shoes to my place, I will give you so much"—she said, "I don't know what I shall do with my little business, for my children are starving"—I said, "If you have anything you can send it me, I will allow you the profits out of it"—the prisoners were in prison four or five days—they were bailed by the Magistrate—I did not give Norris into custody immediately—I saw these shoes hanging in his shop quite exposed—any one could see them—they had only been there about one day—I gave Norris in charge the same afternoon, and Homewood the same day—he was discharged on the 6th of July—I have borrowed shoes—it is a very uncommon thing—I have borrowed shoes of my brother-in-law—Homewood lent them to me when he had an order to do so—Kent was present when Homewood said to Norris, "Say you borrowed them"—Homewood has got a forge now, and is working as a farrier, nearly half-a-mile from me—I do not think he has got any of my customers—he said, "I have neither sold nor made away with your property"—I said, "You have; here is a proof; some of my own make, I made a few days ago"—he denied it—I said I would send for a policeman, and Homewood said to Norris, "Say you borrowed them"—I was within five or six feet, walking away, and Homewood Raid, "Master, come back, don't be spiteful, I will tell you all about it"—I had some beer in the course of the day at the Oxford Arms—I had none with the prisoners—Homewood was not in my service after I made the charge.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant R 15.) About nine o'clock in the morning of the 15th of July, Homewood came to me—I was by the station—he said, "I have come to give myself up; I understand my master has charged me with stealing some shoes; I have lent some to Norris, at Greenwich; the reason I made my escape last night was, I was to ill I did not to be locked up all night in the station-house,"
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known Homewood? A. About twenty years—he was a very hard-working, industrious, sober man, and has a family.
JAMBS KENT . I am a farrier—I was in the prosecutor's employ. One morning, I (cannot remember the day,) my master came to the shop with a man named Norris—he called me from the fire at the other side of the shop, where I was working, and pulled this pair of shoes out of his pocket, and said, "Do you know anything of these, Jem?"—I said, "They look very much like your make"—he said, "You can swear to them?"—I said I could not—he then pulled out a crooked horse-shoe—I said, "That looks like Homewood's make"—he said, "You can swear to this?"—I said, "No"—we then went to my fire, and he said, "Put a private mark on them," which I did—we went out into the back-yard, and called Homewood out of the house—the prosecutor presented this pair of poney-shoes, and said, "Do you know anything of these shoes, Homewood?"—he said, "No, I do not"—the prosecutor turned round from him, and said, "It is my property, you have robbed me of them"—Homewood denied it, and said he had not—Norris did not say anything—the prosecutor told me to fetch a policeman—I did not go, and then he sung out to a man who was working in the shop, named Bill, and said, "Go and fetch a policeman," but some one called him back—the prosecutor was then going away, and Homewood said to Norris, "Just call him back, and we will tell him the whole truth"—he called him back—the prosecutor turned round, and Homewood said, "Now, master, don't be spiteful; come here, I will tell you all about it; I lent these, few pairs of shoes to, this man, till such time as he got his place up, and then he will pay you again"—I did not hear Homewood say anything to Norris more than I have said that I am confident of.
JOHN BOVIS ( police-constable R 139.) On the 11th of July I went with the prosecutor to Norris's shop—the prosecutor took down four pairs of shoes which were hanging on a nail, and said they were his property—Norris did not say anything—the prosecutor said he should give Norris in charge—I said to Norris, "Do you know what you are charged with?"—he said, "I should like to know"—I said, "You are charged with receiving these shoes, knowing them to be stolen"—he said, "Very well."
HENKT DUNFORD . I am ao auctioneer at Greenwich. On the 11th of July I saw the prosecutor and Norris—the prosecutor said to him, "Yes have got some of my property in your shop, I saw them"—Norris said he had not, he had nothing there but his own property—the prosecutor said he had, and then Norris said, "There is such a thing as a, pair of shoes in the shop, I bought them of Homewood."
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you were not before the Magistrate? A. No—Norris was my tenant for two years.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
