2171. PETER BUSHELL was indicted for stealing 1 copper, value 10 s., the goods of John Evans, and fixed to a building.
JOHN EVANS . I live in Prospect-place. I lost a copper from the kitchen of No. 3, Hertford-place, West-street, Camberwell—I saw it safe on the morning of the 20th of June—it is the house of Mr. Cox—I was the tenant—I missed it on the 4th of July, and saw it on the 8th of July—the policeman brought two coppers, one of which fitted my place as near as possible.
JAMES COX . I saw a copper at Union Hall, doubled up, and the bottom cut out—there were three coppers doubled up and. quite disfigured—they were beaten out, and I saw one of them tried to this house—it fitted, except leaving a margin in the cement—there was no cement on the copper.
WILLIAM BURTON ( policeman.) I stopped the prisoner on the 1st of July, at half-past six o'clock, in Kennington-road, about a quarter of a mile from this house, with a basket containing the greater part of three coppers.—as we were going to the station he said, "You think you will do a great deal now, but I can prove where I got them,"and at the station he told the inspector the same, but said he should defer that to another place—one copper was beat out by a smith, and fitted the cement so at to leave a very slight vacancy—the bottom was cut out.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440819-2172]
2172. PETER BUSHELL was again, indicted for stealing 1 copper, value 8 s., the goods of Richard Thomas Thornton Brown, and fixed to a building; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM BROWK . I went to No. 9, Alpha-terrace, a few days after the 24th of June, and saw a copper in the kitchen, the property of Richard Thomas Thornton Brown—there was a sort of whitewash on the top of it, as the house was under repair.
ALFRED JONES . I live in Skinner-street, Bishopsgate, and am employed at Mr. Brown's house, Alpha-terrace, Wawortb—I saw a copper in the back kitchen of No. 3—I was in the habit of leaving my tools in the copper—there was a holdfast in the bottom of it, and the damp had caused it to rust and corrode on the bottom, and by that I should know it again—I saw a copper found on the prisoner which had the mark of which I speak—I saw it fitted to the kitchen—it fitted exactly—the kitchen had been coloured a
salmon-colour, and there was a little of the same colour on the copper—I have no doubt it was the same.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. You would not swear to it? A. Yes I do—I am a bricklayer—I saw it safe on Friday, the 28th of June—I was at work there colouring—I saw the mark which the holdfast made—I had found it lying in the copper, and took it out—the kitchen walls were salmon coloured—I have not a doubt of it—it fitted exactly.
WILLIAM BURTON ( policeman.) I found this copper on the prisoner in a basket on the 1st of July, about 300 yards from this bouse, at half-past six in the morning.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it in the public road you saw him? A. I followed him, seeing him with a basket—the copper was doubled up; but a policeman, who had been a blacksmith, beat it out—I went to the prisoner's house about half-past seven o'clock, and saw his wife—I did not say it would be a long time before she saw him again, nor use the word lag —the prisoner did not tell me he had bought the copper.
MR. CHARNOCK called
ROBERT CHAPMAN . I live in Peacock-street, Newington-butts, and have lived there thirty years—the property is my own, and is leasehold—I have known the prisoner six or seven years—I had been to the Borough to look at a horse for a gentleman, and on returning I met him by the Elephant and Castle on the 29th of June, I think, at half-past six o'clock—a man came after him with some copper, lead, and different things, in a bag at the back of the Elephant and Castle—he took the copper out—it was bent up like a hat-box—he said he was going to sell it at Haman's, who keeps a marine store shop at Wai worth—the prisoner said, "You cannot sell it there, they don't do business this day"(I believe Haman is a Jew)—the prisoner said, "I will buy it of you, if we can agree"—he looked at them, and offered 15 s. for the whole, if he would come to where he lived, as he had not the money about him—they went there, and the prisoner gave him half a sovereign and I think a crown-piece—the copper was all beat up together—there appeared more than one—after he paid him I believe they had some beer—I left them there—I did not examine the articles.
COURT. Q. I suppose you weighed it, did not you? A. No, I believe they weighed it—the man said there was 301bs. of lead—I did not see any lead—I saw the coppers—it was a bushel bag, I think—the man was on the public road on the pavement—there are always a great many people there—nobody interfered—the Elephant was in my way home—the prisoner lives about ten minutes walk from the Elephant, about five minutes walk from where the copper was stolen from—the man was accidentally passing with it—I did not know him, and do not suppose that the prisoner did—he called him by a name—the prisoner is a respectable man—I never knew him in trouble—he is a painter and glazier—I swear all this took place in the public street—I am speaking the truth.
WILLIAM FENSOM ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from the clerk of the peace of Surrey—I was present when he was convicted at Newington sessions for stealing a copper, and sentenced to six months' imprisonment—( read)—I know him to be the man.
GUILTY .* Aged 30.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2173. FRANCIS RYDER was indicted for stealing, at Lambeth, 15 sovereigns, 5 crown-pieces, 12 half-crowns, 5 shillings; 6 spoons, value 18 s.; 1 watch, 2 l. 5 s.; 5 handkerchiefs, 10 s.; and I brooch, 5 s.; the property of William Edmonds, in his dwelling-house.
WILLIAM EDMONDS . I keep a beer-shop in Skinner-street, St. Mary, Lambeth, and keep the house. MATTHEW HOPKINS . I am a labourer, and know the prisoner well. On Sunday, the 21st of July, I saw him, and said, "You have got new clothes"—he said, "Yes, I bought them down the New-cut; we have been to Edmonds's, and had some money there;" he said they had done a job there, and took four silk handkerchiefs, six silver spoons, a pin or brooch, and about 9 l. in money; that he got it up stairs in a big chest in the back room.
MARY ANN TAYLOR . I am the wife of Joseph Taylor, and live opposite the prosecutor's. I was at my door on Friday, the 19th, about half-past five o'clock, and saw the prisoner run out of Edmonds's door, and run up the street—somebody ran after him—I swear he is the man.
WILLIAM BURTON ( policeman.) [trialAccount t18440819-2174]
2174. ROGER CREADON was indicted for feloniously cutting and wounding Robert Butter worth on his head, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. SIMON conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT BUTTERWORTH . I am a carman. On the 15th of July I went to the Three Compasses, in Little Guildford-street, St. Saviour's, for some beer for supper—I had a jug with me—Mr. Cole and Mr. Parsons were there—I drank some beer with them—while standing there the prisoner came in and called for a pint of beer—Mr. Peacock, the landlord, drew it—he put his hand into his pocket and pulled out some halfpence; it was not sufficient; he then pulled out an old coin—the landlord said he did not take such money as that—he drank the beer, and wanted another pint—the landlord refused—he struck the pot on the counter twice—the landlord came round from the bar, turned him out, and closed the door on him—about five minutes afterwards he came back and rapped at the door—I, being close to it, opened it—I was going home, and before I could get away from him he cut me on the head with a pair of tongs, and I fell senseless on the ground—when I came to myself I was at the station-house—I was hurt on my head, my left knee, and both my legs—the skin of my head was broken—it bled a great deal—a surgeon's apprentice attended me for about ten minutes—I did not go to him again—I had never seen or spoken to the prisoner before.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. I suppose he was very tipsy?
A. Not very—I did not perceive that he staggered—I was not tipsy—I went to my work as usual next day, still I was in great pain—I did not strike the prisoner—I do not know that his jaw was broken—it was not broken when he came in, that I know of—he had no bandage on—he has one on now—I saw him at the bar at Union-hall—he had no bandage on his head then—I did not see any blood on his face.
CHARLES COLE . I am a job-master, and live in Little Guildford-street. I was at the Three Compasses—I did not see the prisoner come in—I saw him come out of the tap-room—no one interrupted or said anything to him—he asked for a pint of beer—he had got a little bit of tin in his hand, which the landlord refused to take—he then turned out with a little tiff —in about ten minutes he came in again, took a pint pot up, and cut three holes in the counter—the landlord was sent for—he came out of the parlour, and the prisoner was put out of the door—in about ten minutes or a quarter of as hour he came back, and beat the door three or four times, apparently with a weapon—the prosecutor went to carry a pot of beer home for his supper, and he caught the weapon on his head—I heard a loud cry for me—I stepped out immediately—I saw Butterworth lying on the ground, and the prisoner beating him with the tongs—he struck him in my presence—I went and took him by the collar, put my foot behind him, and he fell in the gutter—I immediately took the tongs and pushed them into the street—a man took them into the public-house—they were very heavy—he had gone home for them to his own house—when I went up to Butterworth the blood was streaming down on the flag-stone—if I had not rescued him he would have been seriously injured—he was insensible for about an hour and twenty minutes—he was wounded all about his face—we took him on a stretcher to the station—a surgeon was sent for, who washed and dressed the wound—it bled a great deal—the blood lay next morning on the stones—this is the weapon—( produced.)
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner lives within a door or two of this house? A. I understand so—I do not think he was very far gone in liquor—I would not swear he was not—the landlord had not spoken cross to him, or any one else in the house—if he had not put him out, we should all have been cut to pieces with this pint pot—I was sober—I had not been in the house more than twenty minutes—I thought it right to assist the man—I did not strike the prisoner—I do not think he had his head tied up when he came into the Compasses—I saw him next day at Union-hall—he appeared as if he had got a bruise at the side of his face—I did not notice that it was very much swollen—I did not give it him—I cannot say whether the prosecutor did—I do not know that his jaw was broken on this occasion—I have heard strange tales about it—his face was not tied up at Union-hall, that I saw, nor bloody, or his shirt, that I noticed—I did not strike or kick him.
JOHN MENHINICK ( police-constable M 197.) On the 15th of July I saw the prisoner and prosecutor both lying on the ground—the prosecutor was bleeding profusely from a cut on the temple—I took the prisoner to the station—I saw this pair of tongs—the prisoner told me next morning that they were his.
Cross-examined. Q. When you went up it was all over? A. Yes—the prisoner did not appear to be in pain—he said, "Do you want me?"—I did not see any blood—I cannot say how he came in the state he is in now—I saw him at the Magistrate's office—he had a blow in the face, bat it was not tied up at all—I do not know that he has had a broken jaw—I have heard that he has been under the care of Mr. Maynard, in the gaol, ever since he has been there.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 30.— Confined Six Weeks.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440819-2175]
2175. WILLIAM BUTLER was indicted for embezzlement.
JOSEPH TOOTON . The prisoner was about three weeks in my service—I employed him to receive money—I sent him with some gin to Gayton's bouse on three several days—he was to receive the money for it, and account to me for it every night—he did not account to me on the 18th or 15th of June for any money he received—I have had no communication with him on the subject—he left me in consequence of getting in liquor, and this was discovered afterwards.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. It was his duty to carry out beer and gin, and you looked to him for the money? A. Fbr what he sold—there are a good many customers I know nothing about—I gave him 1 s. or 2 s. worth of copper every morning to give change—he had to account to me for that, and the money he received for gin and beer—I looked to him for the value of what he took out, unless he returned it, or booked it to somebody I knew—I sent him to the brewery at Wandsworth on Monday, at one o'clock, to order some beer—he did not come back till eleven o'clock on Tuesday—I understand he got drunk—he said he had lost and spent the money—he begged of me to allow him to work it out—I said no, I would not keep him—I gave him two hours to get away, but he did not choose to go, and I gave him into custody—I cannot say he was sober then.
MARTHA GAYTON . I am the wife of George Gayton. I do not recollect paying any money to the prisoner on the 13th and 15th of June—I deal with the prosecutor, and buy beer and gin, or whatever I want of the prisoner—I always paid him for what I had, and never missed doing so—if I had any gin on the 13th or 15th of June, I paid him, but I kept no account of the time—my husband and I have a little gin and water of a night.
Cross-examined. Q. If you had the gin he had the money? A. Yes—if I had any on the 15th I did not have more on the 17th—I am certain of that—I have no recollection of having any gin about the 13th of June, but I am positive he never went away without the money—I cannot say that I gave him any specific sum in that week—I cannot say that I paid him 1 s. 4 d.—he came every day with beer and gin if anybody wants it—there was no understanding with the prisoner to keep it a secret what I had—my husband knew of my having the gin, and shared it—I never told the prisoner to book it, for I paid him the money.
JOSEPH TOOTON re-examined. He booked a pint of gin on the 13th, I think, to Mrs. Gayton—I cannot tell to whom he delivered it, but he was that much short, and booked it to Mrs. Gayton.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440819-2176]
2176. WILLIAM BUTLER was again indicted for embezzlement.
JOSEPH TOOTON . The prisoner booked a pint of gin on the 14th and 31st of May and 7th of June—I have a memorandum from my book—he told me to book this quantity of gin to Mrs. Gayton on these several dates, 1 s. 4 d., each day—I do not recollect whether he represented, on those several days, that he had supplied that quantity of gin, and that it was not paid—I do not keep these things in my mind—I did not know my book was required here.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Wightman.
[trialAccount t18440819-2177]
2177. JAMES CARROLL was indicted for the wilful murder of Elizabeth Hobbs.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH BRAY . My husband is a tailor. I live next door to the prisoner in Birdcage-alley, Borough. On the 2nd of March, about a quarter-past nine o'clock in the morning, I was hanging my things in the yard, and heard a terrible fighting in the second floor in the next house—the prisoner keeps that house, and lets lodgings—he hawks things in the street—I never saw Mrs. Hobbs but once, but I know she lived on the second floor of that house—I went and knocked at the street door, and demanded it to be opened (as I heard screams of murder) four or five times—it was then opened by a woman—I said, "What is Mr. Carroll doing up stairs?"—the door was slammed in my face—I went into my yard again, heard the blows repeated, and "Murder" called—I demanded the door to be opened again, and saw the prisoner standing on the stairs, and Mrs. Carroll by the deceased's side—I said, "What are you about? if you don't let the woman come out, I will fetch a policeman"—he told me to mind my own business—I saw Mrs. Hobbs come down stain with a blow on her forehead, and the blood trickling down her neck—she called the prisoner many bad names, and used many bad expressions—she asked him for her boots and her husband's—Mrs. Carroll said, "Betsy, I will give them to you when you have paid for the crockery you have broken"—the deceased called her several names, and continued calling out for revenge, twenty times over, and aggravated them very much—they both used bad words—he said if she did not leave him alone, he would cut her head off—she had a fork in her hand, beating it up and down—I did not see anything in the prisoner's hand—the door was slammed in my face again—I went indoors, went out again, and heard her call him bad names, and say, "Carroll, don't strike me with the poker, I am not a man, but a woman."
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was not the deceased of extremely drunken habits? A. I think she was in liquor that morning—the fork was the size of a dinner-fork—she was beating it up and down the stairs at the prisoner, and aggravating him very much.
WILLIAM ALLEN . I am a coal-porter. I lived with Mrs. Carroll. On the 2nd of March I heard a terrible noise in the two-pair room, where Mr. and Mrs. Hobbs lived—I lodged on the third floor, over their room—I heard the cry of "Murder" two or three times—I came down stairs, and found Carroll standing on the third or fourth stair down from the landing—Mrs. Hobbs was standing on the landing, a very little distance from him—she had a fork in her hand, and tried to job it at him—she made several attempts to run it into him—Carroll said, "If I had a poker I would knock your head off"—Mrs. Carroll was not by then—I saw Sarah Bray down stairs in the street—when I came down, I said to Carroll, "Come down stairs with me," and he followed me down stairs—I passed Mrs. Hobbs on the landing—after Carroll had been down stairs about half an hour, Mrs. Hobbs came down stain, and called him all manner of names—he went out—Mrs. Hobbs went up stairs again—Carroll came back again, and there was another row —she wanted her boots—he said when she paid for the things, he would give her the boots, and all at once he called out, "She has run the fork into my eye"—he ran to the fire-place and took up a piece of broken tongs, and ran into the passage where Mrs. Hobbs was standing—I did not see him strike a blow—I did not see Mrs. Hobbs after that till half an hour after, when I found her dead in the room—I did not go into the passage—I was binding my arm up—I had had my arm broken—I did not hear any blow struck, but heard her hallooing at him, "You murdering old curse," and all manner of things.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you notice the prisoner's face after he called out that she had struck him with the fork? A. No—I did not see any blood
about his person—the deceased was not a very stout woman—I hod seen her four or five times—I never heard of her being drunk.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Did you see the prisoner afterwards? A. Yes—he went off for the doctor himself.
COURT. Q. Did you see any mark on the deceased's face? A. I saw blood trickling on the left side of her neck as I went down on the landing—after she was dead I saw several marks on her forehead—Mrs. Bray came to the door and looked—Carroll opened it, and Mrs. Bray said, "Don't kill the woman"—Mr. Carroll said, "I have sent for a policeman, and the policeman won't come, what am I to do with her?"—this was after I had passed her on the landing.
WILLIAM HOBBS . I am the husband of the deceased. On Saturday, the 2nd of March, I got up about half-past five o'clock, and went out with the deceased to go to work—we went into a public-house, and took half a quartern of rum—I drank the biggest part of it, and my wife the rest—she left me about five minutes before six—she was then in a very good state of health, and was sober—her name was Elizabeth Hobbs—I went to my work, and knew nothing more till I came home at one o'clock.
SARAH SUMMERS . I am the wife of William Summers, and live in Birdcage-alley, the next house to the prisoner, in the first floor room. Between nine and ten o'clock in the morning of the 2nd of March I heard a terrible crying in the prisoner's house—I went down stairs, and then up stairs into the two-pair, and looked through a hole—I saw Mr. and Mrs. Carroll in the room, and Mrs. Hobbs—I heard her call him a transported (using a bad word)—directly she did so, Mrs. Carroll struck her with her hand—I could not say whether it was shut or open—she repeated the same word over again, and then Carroll struck her with his fist or hand, I cannot say which—he had nothing in his hand—I saw no more—I weir down stairs, and called Mrs. Bass.
MARY BASS . I live in Birdcage-alley. In consequence of what Mrs. Summers told me I went to the house, and saw Mrs. Hobbs lying on the bed very much swollen—I saw nothing happen between her and the prisoner.
MARY BURN . I was living in Birdcage-walk. On the 2nd of March, between nine and ten o'clock, I was at Carroll's house—I saw the deceased come up stairs—Mrs. Carroll was coming up, and got quarrelling with her—I heard nothing more till after Mr. and Mrs. Carroll went up stairs into her room—I heard a disturbance over head, but 1 was not up there at the time—it was like falling—I heard the deceased say if her husband had been at home she would not have been ill-used so—I went up stairs after that, and she was bleeding behind the ear—Carroll was in the room at the time—I saw no blow struck.
Cross-exarnined. Q. You were a friend of Mrs. Hobbs? A. Yes—she and I had not been drinking that morning—I had not seen her that morning—I saw her the night before, and parted with her in the Borough, about one o'clock—I will swear it was not three—we had nothing to drink.
WILLIAM RENDLE . I am parish surgeon of St. George's, and live in Blackman-street, Borough. I was called in to see Mrs. Hobbs about ten o'clock in the morning—she was lying on the bed dead—I made a post mortem examination on Monday morning—I found a slight abrasion on the right elbow, not of any importance; a bruise on the left cheek just under the eye and above the eye, and one on the temple—there was a wound behind the right ear about an inch long—on further examination all those bruises and the wound corresponded, with a large quantity of blood clotted within the skin between that and the bone—I found the bone in no way injured—I found the dura mater very full of blood, more than usual, and the membranes containing
the brain were more than usually turgid with blood—the brain was not particularly turgid, and in no way injured—the heart, lungs, chest, stomach, and bowels were all perfectly healthy—the stomach contained nothing more than is usually found, just the remains of a meal—I attribute her death to concussion of the brain—the brain was turgid with excitement, and the covering of the brain was turgid, producing pressure on it—in my opinion the concussion was produced by repeated blows —it is usual in great excitement for large quantities of blood to flow to the brain—in my judgment the cause of death was from conjoint pressure on the brain, the large flow of blood, and the concussion from repeated blows.
Cross-examined. Q. Concussion may be occasioned by other means than repeated blows? A. It may—a fall would produce concussion, excitement alone would not—a person being drunk would be more likely to have the blood become turgid—the blow on the forehead was a severe one—I should say that could not cause the appearances about the brain, because the vessels were equally full all over the brain—I attribute it to concussion generally, and turgidity—one blow joined to the excessive excitement might have produced the concussion, and caused the death.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Could the blow behind the right ear have been produced by a fall? A. No—that was produced by an instrument—the state of the brain could certainly not have been produced by a fall—I should say that Carroll and his wife both fetched me, and seemed very anxious that I should go to see the deceased—he held up his arm, and complained that she had hurt it—I did not see any blow about him—he said nothing about his lip.
COURT. Q. You saw a wound, would that account for the death of the deceased? A. I consider the whole of the wounds and injuries together—not that alone—the concussion might be occasioned by a fall.
ROBERT STURGEON ( policeman). I was called in to the deceased's house on the 2nd of March. I took the prisoner's wife into custody—I did not see the prisoner till the 16th of July, when he was in custody—he was taken in Kent-street, Borough—I had been frequently to look after him at his house—I produce part of a pair of tongs which was brought to the workhouse where the inquest was held, and I found another part in the room where the deceased was lying, standing up in the fire place.
WILLIAM ALLEN re-examined. This is the piece of the tongs he picked up.
GUILTY of Manslaughter. Aged 67.— Recommended to mercy in consequence of the provocation.— Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Justice Wightman.
[trialAccount t18440819-2178]
2178. FAITH BOWERMAN was indicted for the wilful murder of her female child.
MR. SIMON conducted the Prosecution.
ANN BOWERMAN . I am the wife of Joseph Bowerman, the prisoner's brother, and live in Belvidere-road, Lambeth. The prisoner came to reside with us three or four months before the 1st of July—I had no reason to suspect that she was in the family way—on the morning of the 1st of July, about half-past eight o'clock I went out for some salts for the prisoner, as she told me she was not very well—I did not recommend her to take medicine—she told me her sister had given it to her when she was ill—I was gone about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour at the outside—I had a key and let myself in—I found the prisoner standing at the foot of the stairs—I observed a great deal of blood in the passage and on the stairs-—I told her 1 thought she had been delivered of a child—she positively denied it and said she had not, that
it was the regular course of nature—I have a child myself—I advised her to go to bed, but she would not—I went and fetched my husband—I was absent I should say nearly an hour—shortly after my return a policeman came to the house—I saw nothing of any child—I do not know whether she had prepared any linen—there was plenty of mine about.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. I suppose you have not looked about that? A. No—my child is twelve months old—the prisoner was particularly kind to it, and was a very good girl indeed—I have been married two years.
EDWARD JAMES MOODY . I worked for Mr. Patrick at the time in question and live in the Waterloo-road. On the morning of the 1st of July, when I was going to work down to my master's yard, I saw something lying just outside the gate of Mr. Patrick's yard—there are two gates, and this was the inner one—it is a wide road—there are two yards within one large gate—the wall of Mr. Bowerman's house runs down by the side of the gate—I did not know what it was, but on going near to it I thought it was a child—I communicated what I had seen to a person named Glaire—I went quite close to it—I thought it was a child—it was naked—it was lying about two yards from the wall.
Cross-examined. Q. What time in the morning was it? A. A quarter to nine o'clock—it was two or three yards from the side of the wall, some distance from the wall—this gateway is a private way—it is open for any one that works for Mr. Patrick—not for strangers to pass through—people do not go down there—it does not lead anywhere—it is all open there—there is no covering at all—I should say it is as wide as thit Court—anybody in the main road could see down the road and see what I saw—it was six, seven, or eight yards from the main road—I did not see any other person there—there is an outer gate which comes into the Belvidere-road—that is open before six o'clock.
EDMUND GLAIRE . I am one of Mr. Patrick's labourers. On the 1st of July I received a communication from Moody, in consequence of which I went into the roadway leading to my master's premises, and saw a new born female child—I picked it up and put it in my apron—the navel string was exposed, and through that I observed its not being many moments born—on the way to the doctor's I looked at it, and it was foaming at the mouth—I observed a lump on the side of its head, and the shoulder very much disfigured—I took it to Dr. Carruthers's shop, by the York-road, Lambeth—a policeman was sent for—the child was alive.
JOHN COLLISON ( police-constable N 427.) On the 1st of July I saw Glaire go into a doctor's shop—I followed him and saw the cbiwlaid on the table in the doctor's parlour—it was a female new born infant—part of the skull appeared driven in—I took it up to the workhouse—it died about two hours after—I have been to Mr. Bowerman's premises—the yard from the house to the bottom is twenty-eight feet long—the wall is twelve feet high next the house, but the nearest part to where the child was is ten feet inside and six feet three inches without, where the child was lying—I was not present when it was picked up.
HENRY JAMES HART ( police-constable L 58.) On the 1st of July, in consequence of information, I went to Mr. Patrick's roadway—I observed some blood on a stone in the road, about three yards from the wall—I looked over the wall, and saw a quantity of blood in the yard—I went round to Mr. Bowerman's door, and knocked—the prisoner opened the door with a child in her arms, which appeared about twelve months old—this was a little after ten o'clock—I asked her if there had been a child born in the yard or in the
house that morning—she said no, it was not her—I asked her if she knew anything of it—she said no, she did not—her apron and hands were covered with blood—I called her attention to that fact—I left a man in charge of the door, and went to the station, and communicated with the inspector on duty—he came back to the house with me—I was sent for a doctor—the prisoner seemed greatly agitated, and very weakly—on a subsequent occasion I examined the premises, and saw an after-birth found in the dust-hole—I saw it put into a basket, and taken to the workhouse by James Brook, L 118—he is not here.
Cross-examined. Q. You say you saw some blood on a stone in Patrick's yard? A. In the gateway of the yard, about the length of this Court from the Belvidere-road—one road leads into another person's road, and Mr. Patrick's also—the stone was right in the middle of the gateway, about twelve yards out of the Belvidere-road, I should think—I did not measure it—the after-birth was found during the time the doctor was in the parlour, about ten o'clock the same morning—I saw it found—the dust-hole is about three yards from the kitchen—the stone I saw with the blood on was about three yards from the wall—I did not measure it—it appeared to be a piece of paving-stone, lying in the ground.
MRS. BOWERMAN re-examined. When I went out that morning, I left no one in the house but the prisoner and my baby, no servant.
OLIVER DUKE . I am the surgeon of Lambeth workhouse. On the 1st of July a messenger from the workhouse came to fetch me—I went, and saw the child—it was in the possession of a nurse—the head appeared very much contused, the bones of the skull appeared fractured, and the back of the child was bruised very much—it lived about two hours after I saw it—an after-birth was also produced to me, I think by Hart—in my judgment, the bruise on the head caused the death—the other bruises were not of a very serious nature.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know the age of the prisoner? A. I believe she is about nineteen—the head is the first part that makes its appearance in the world, in a confinement—I should think the bruises were not such as would arise from the child striking its head against the stairs, if suspended from the mother—striking the head against the stairs, or any hard substance, would produce the appearances I saw—the loss of blood, if the mother delivered herself, would very much affect her mind—it might affect the brain, so as to produce almost frenzy—I should say a mother so young as seventeen or eighteen would have great difficulty in delivering herself—it is very doubtful how long it would take for a girl of her age to deliver herself, it depends on the obstacles met with—supposing the pains of labour had not come on, I should think it would be impossible for a person to be delivered under some hours—I cannot say—I examined the person of the prisoner when she came into the hospital, about two hours after I had seen the child—there were symptoms of her having been delivered.
GUILTY of Manslaughter. Aged 17.— Confined Two Years.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18440819-2179]
2179. AMELIA ALFERY was indicted for feloniously attempting to drown Mary Sarah French, with intent to murder her:—Other COUNTS, calling her Mary French.
WILLIAM ALFERY . I do not know how old I am—the prisoner is my sister—I live with my father, by the White Horse, on the water side, Wandsworth. On the 29th of July I was on Wandsworth-common—I saw the prisoner there by herself, at a place called Black Sea—there was plenty there—she
had her children with her, a little girl and a boy—their names were Henry French and Mary French—she has lived with a man of the name of French, and the children were called by his name—I went away for some time, and came back to play at cricket—I did not see her then—when I went over in the afternoon, I saw her walking about, and the children with her—I do not know what time it was—I did not see her do anything—I heard her scream—she was then in the water, in a pond there—the children were with her—I do not know whether they were tied to her—I went in—it was very shallow water—I held up the children's heads, and tried to hold her head up too—I kept the children's heads above water till somebody came—I saw her before I saw her in the water, and asked her to go home with me, and she told me to go home—I did not see her sitting on a bench, I saw her walking about—I did not hear her say anything to the children—I saw them with her before I saw them in the water—I did not hear her call to them—I did not see her do anything with a shawl before she went into the water—I did see her go into the water—I do not know how the children were taken into the water—I was not watching her—I saw her go in—I think the children walked in—nobody has talked to me about this since I was before the Magistrate—I think the children walked into the water—I did not see them go in—I did not see her carry them—I was looking another way when she went in.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Do you mean to represent that you actually saw her go into the water with the children, or saw her after she was in? A. I saw her after she was in the water, I did not see her go in—I saw her sitting on the bench—I do not know what time of day it was—it was in the afternoon—there was plenty of others there, fishing, and running all round when I saw her first—I left her, went home, and then returned again to play at cricket—I stopped at home about ten minutes—it was her scream that I heard—I heard somebody in the water, I did not know who it was, and I ran over—I cannot say whether it was the children's scream or the prisoner's—the water was just up to my knees—she has been in confinement.
GEORGE STEER . I am gardener to Mr. Wilson, of Wandsworth-common. On the evening of the 29th of July I heard a scream, went to the pond, and found the prisoner and her two children in the water—the little boy (Alfrey) was then endeavouring to keep the children's heads above water—I assisted, and got them out—the children were placed one on each side of the prisoner—one was tied to her body with a shawl, but in my flurry I did not see whether the other was or not—the prisoner was lying on her back in the water—it did not cover her face at that moment—she was very much exhausted indeed—the only thing I heard her say was "My children," in a very low tone—I got them all out of the water, and they were conveyed to the workhouse—I untied the knot of the shawl—it was a double knot, by which the children were fastened to her.
Cross-examined. Q. It was the mother's scream you heard, was it not? A. It was the four screams—the whole of them screamed—I did not particularly distinguish the mother's scream—the place where I saw them was very shallow at that particular time—some parts of the pond are deep—the part where they were is not more than a foot deep, and for a considerable distance round—I think she was not sensible—she was very much frightened indeed, her hands and finger-nails changed quite into a blue state—the only thing she said was, "My children," so very low that no one but myself heard it I think—I assisted in taking her part of the way to the workhouse, but could not maintain my strength to carry her all the way—I know nothing of her circumstances, or of the man French, or of her having been in a place of confinement, only from what I have heard.
WILLIAM LEE ( policeman.) I was alarmed and came to the pond—the prisoner was then out of the pond, supported by Steer—she appeared in a very exhausted state—I assisted to move her to the workhouse—I have the shawl.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe she was scarcely able to articulate? A. No—I was at the station-house—she did not say anything there about her children—all I heard her say was, it was trouble—she did not say what trouble—she did not say, "O, my children, O, my children!"—she was in a very weak state—I saw no letter.
EMILY ALFERY . I am the mother of the prisoner—she is not married—she has for six years lived with a man of the name of French, and has had two children by him—he has been in prison lately, and since he came out of prison he has deserted her—in consequence of that she has been ill, and has been confined six weeks in a Lunatic Asylum—I fetched her out, and took her to my house about three months ago—I had observed lately, before this day, symptoms of her being ill again—she became very poorly—I thought her head was getting wrong again sometimes.
JOHN BUSAIN ( police-inspector.) I took the prisoner in charge—I have a letter which I received in Court—I saw it handed from the Governor of the workhouse into the hands of the Magistrate's clerk, and from him it was sent to me—I put a mark on it—I told the prisoner she was charged with attempting to murder her two children, and attempting to commit suicide—she said very faintly to me, "It was distress that made me do it."
EMILY ALFERY re-examined, I saw this letter taken from my daughter at the workhouse—it is from French—she had received that since she came from the asylum—it was after the receipt of that letter that I observed the symptoms of her head being wrong again.
( Letter read)—" AMELIA,—I understand that you have been at my sister's, inquiring after me; and I should be much obliged to you if you would not trouble my sister, for I think she has had trouble enough already. You need not trouble yourself in seeking after me, for I don't mean to have any more to do with you or any other woman at present. I will do what I can for the children; (God bless them!) but unfortunately it is out of my power at present to do any more than I am sending. I have had to walk a great many miles in seeking employment; whereas if you had let me have been at T. Morison's a little while longer, till I had got into work, which I had a good prospect of summer work; whereas if you had let me be, we might have been comfortable now. But where is the man that will stand the hunting as I did, which you are now hunting me night and day, and actually drove me from my native land, where I meant to be, unless I have to come, on particular occasions, to Wandsworth. Make up your mind for industry; and throw off that independent spirit which is your enemy. If you have any thing to say to me you can send word by J. Cremer, where I shall be, at his place, one day in the week, as I have little or nothing to do. No more at present. Adieu.
" H. FRENCH."
MR. CHARNOCK to EMILY ALFERY. Q. Was she a kind and affectionate mother to her children? A. Yes—she interested herself very much about the trouble French got into, and went about everywhere to get him a character, and get him a very light sentence—she travelled night and day for him—this letter was found in her bosom when she was taken from the water—it fell out in my presence, when they were taking her clothes off—I never saw anythingin her conduct to the children but that of a kind and affectionate mother—after French deserted her I took her and her children, and did my best—she has
been very unhappy since the receipt of that letter—she was six weeks in Garrett's Lunatic Asylum.
NOT GUILTY, being of unsound mind.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440819-2180]
2180. JOHANNA BUCKLEY was indicted for stealing 1 cash-box, value 1 l.; 10 s.; 160 sovereigns, 20 half-sovereigns, and 3 5 l.; Bank-notes, the property of Charles Patrick Connelly, in his dwelling-house; and CATHERINE WIGZELL for feloniously receiving the same, well-knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES PATRICK CONNELLY . I am a newsvender, at the Railway station, London-bridge, and live in Duke-street, Southwark. In March last I had a cash-box in my bed-room containing 170 l.; in gold, and three 5 l.; notes, one of which was No. 61086—I took this memorandum from the note when I received it from a customer—I know Buckley—in Nov. last she requested me to lend her 1 l.;, to relieve her from some embarrassments which she was then in, and she would pay me in a fortnight—I lent it her, but she did not repay me—in Dec. she met me in the Borough, and asked me for the loan of 10 s. to get a shawl out of pledge, that she might be enabled to get a situation or earn a better living—I said I would not lend her any more money—she said, "I am really in the most destitute condition"—I said if she was so destitute, she might come to my house, and probably my wife would relieve her, by allowing her to remain in the house, and providing her with victuals—next day, when I went home to dinner, I found her there, and there she remained for five weeks, supported by us—in Jan. she obtained a situation as servant of all-work, in the Kent-road—my wife was very ill, and being in want of a servant, Buckley's sister, Brown, came to us immediately after she left—on the 10th of Feb. the sister applied to me for some money, which I lent her—I then prevented Buckley coming to the house, suspecting she had stolen a handkerchief of mine—she did not come to the house after that with my consent—at the time she was residing with us, her husband was in Ireland—he was a labourer—at the time I lent her the 1 l.;, he was keeping a beer-shop in Francis-street, Westminster-road, and their means there were so insufficient, that Messrs. Thone, the brewers, turned them out because they had not money enough to pay them for the beer—I heard that from Buckley—in March her husband was in the hospital—I was not at home on Friday evening, the 8th of March—I might have been out as late as eleven—I left my wife at home—a lady, named Thompson, was lodging in the house—in consequence of something her servant communicated to me, I made inquiries, and missed my money on Monday the 11th of March, between a quarter and half-past twelve—my wife first missed it—I heard that Buckley had been there on the Friday preceding—nothing was missed between the Friday and the Monday—the last time the cash-box was seen safe was on the Thursday—I know this 5 l.; note now produced, No. 61086—that, with two others which have not been found, was in the cash-box.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. When were you apprized of Buckley having been there? A. On the Monday that the property was missed—I suspected her then—I gave her in charge, on the 4th of July, I think, when I had ascertained that the note had been paid into the Bank of England on the 1st—I have charged five persons altogether with this offence; Mary Quin, Brown, Buckley, and her husband, and Wigzell—I have been four times before the Magistrate—I had all those five persons in the dock—Mr. Cottingham would not admit any of them to bail on the first
occasion, but on the second, Mr. Traill did so—he did not enter into the merits of the case as Mr. Cottingham did—Quin was discharged in the first instance—Buckley and Wigzell were bound over in 50 l. each—I have only preferred a bill against these two—Quin is a relative of the prisoner's, she passed the note to Mrs. Harrison—I do not visit Mrs. Harrison—I was never there before this note was traced—Brown is Buckley's sister—Buckley is no relation of mine—she is a distant relation of my wife's, not a cousin—I believe she is my wife's grandmother's niece—I am an Irishman—when I missed my box I went to Mr. Smith, in Swan-street, Dover-road, the landlord of the house I had previously lived at, to know if I had left it there—I did not think I had done so, but in the agitation I was in, I hardly knew what I was doing—I went there within half an hour after I had heard of the loss—I did not see the landlord—I had only left his place the previous week—the cash-box was in a trunk—I had left it in my wife's possession—I had seen it about three weeks before I lost it, in the trunk—I gave the key of it to my wife—I had not seen it in the house it was stolen from—I had accumulated this money in my busines, as a newsvender, during the three yean I have been at the station—I have done a very extensive business—Brown was with us about six or seven weeks—she slept on a very comfortable sofa, which was an equivalent to a bed—I do not know whether she had bed-clothes, or whether she covered herself with her own clothes, or my coat.
ELIZABETH CONNELLY . I am the prosecutor's wife. I saw my husband's cash-box on Thursday the 7th of March, between twelve and half-past, in a large trunk in our bed-room—the trunk was not locked—the cash-box was—there must have been 180 l.; in it—I cannot say to 5 l.;—you go through our sitting-room into the bed-room—there is a passage door for the use of the house—I was at home on Friday evening, the 8th of March, but I was down in the kitchen some hours—we were lately in the house, and I was busy arranging—a person might go up stairs, go through our sitting-room into the bed-room, and take the cash-box out of the trunk—I am quite sure it was there—I know Buckley was in the greatest distress—she knew exactly where I kept the cash-box—she has seen it there in my presence—Mr. Connelly has put money into it in her presence.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Has he put money into it in other people's presence? A. Not that I am aware of—Mrs. Thompson, her daughter and son, and servant, lodged in the house at that time—no one else, but my husband, myself, and Brown, the servant—Buckley used to come to visit her—Brown slept on a sofa, and had plenty of good clothes to cover her—I have never pledged things myself, or sent Brown, or Buckley to do so, but Buckley took a black satin shawl of mine—Brown one day pledged a shawl of Buckley's, about a month before the robbery—my husband had gone out, and I had forgotten to ask him for any silver—I had none at home, and said, "Dear me, what shall I do?"—she said, "I will go and pledge my shawl"—I said, "O dear, no, I do not require anything of the sort, "but she went, and gave me the money—I gave her back the money, and redeemed the shawl soon after the robbery was found out—she never pledged anything else for me.
GRACE HASTEWELL . I was servant to Mrs. Thompson, and lived in the same house as Mr. Connelly. On Friday, the 8th of March, I saw Buckley there—it was between seven and ten o'clock that I saw her there—the last time was near ten—I opened the door three times during that evening, but only once to Buckley—I told her I could not be bothered to open the door so many times—she told me she would not trouble me any more, but the bolt went in by the slamming of the door—I told her it was impossible it could go in by doing so, it never had done so—she said she would not trouble me
to open it any more if I would leave it open then, she was going up for a parcel, and then she was going out—I did not know of her having left a parcel—I do not know where Mrs. Connelly was at that time—Mrs. Thompson was at home, but neither her son nor daughter—I did not see Buckley go out—I left the door for her—I did not see her go up the stairs, or which way she went—she said she was going up.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. What part of the house do you occupy? A. The four upper rooms and one kitchen—the Connelly's occupied two rooms under us and another kitchen—Brown was servant there at the time—I had only seen Buckley there once before, and that was on the Wednesday previous—I do not know whether she had any parcel there—she was with Brown one part of the time I saw her—I only saw her for a short time.
JANE HARRISON . I live in Wellington-street, Strand. I have seen Buckley once—a relative of hers, named Quin, lived servant with me—I have known Wigzell about a year—on the 29th of June she brought me a note to change—I took the number of it at the time—I scratched it with my fingernail on the back of a letter—I have it here—it is 61086—I sent Mary Quin to get it changed—I believe she is a relation of Wigzell's—while Quin was gone Wigzell said her husband had found it—she afterwards said, "If anything is said about it, for God's sake say it was given to you by a gentleman"—I live at a cigar-shop.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When was this second occasion? A. When the officers came after Quin, about a fortnight after, I think—I went to Wigzell's house on Quin's being taken for passing the note—the note was traced to me, and then I said it was Wigzell gave it me, and so it was—I am single—I do not keep the cigar-shop—I am in it sometimes, and have a sitting-room and bed-room up stairs—I live under the protection of a gentleman, who visits me there—other gentlemen may come sometimes—Harrison is my real name—I have gone by the name of Wilson, but by no other name.
JOHN WILSON . I changed the note for Mary Quin, Mrs. Harrison's servant. She brought it to the bar to my sister, who asked me if I would change it—I said, "Yes," and my sister gave her the 5 l.;—I put it in a little drawer in the bar, and about an hour and a half after paid it to Mr. Summers, of Russell-street—I should not know it again.
JAMES SUMMERS . I received a 5 l.; note from Mr. Wilson. I marked it at the time "John Wilson" on the back—this produced is it—I paid it into Coutts's Bank on the Monday following.
JOHN VANDER KISTE . I am a clerk in the Bank of England. I produce this note—it was brought into the bank by Sutton, one of the out-tellers—I do not know whether it was paid in by a banker.
WILLIAM POCOCK ( police-constable F 81.) I took the prisoners into custody. Buckley lived at No. 1, Hooper-street, Westminster-road—I took her on the evening of the 10th of July in Wellington-street, Strand, in the street—I took her to the station—she was searched by a female—I afterward received a key of a trunk, with which I went to No. 1, Hooper-street, and in searching found a trunk under the counter in the shop—I unlocked it with the key, and found some female wearing apparel and this purse, folded up in one of the articles, containing 10 l.; in gold—I also found some bills for goods purchased, amounting to something under 20 l.;, also this agreement for taking the place—( this was dated the 13th of April, by which James Buckley agreed to take the premises at 5 s. a week—two bills for articles purchased by James Buckley were then read, one dated the 22 nd of April, amounting to 6 l.; 6 s., and the other
the 20 th of April, 7 l.; 10 s.)—there were a few more bills of small amounts of later dates—I have since ascertained that James Buckley left Charing-cross Hospital on the 9th of April—I found a quantity of weights and scales fixtures, and house furniture, to the value of 10 l.; or 15 l.;—Wigzell gave me her address, when I took her, No, 1, Cross-court, Russell-court, Drury-lane—I went there, and found boxes containing a quantity of new table linen, dresses, and clothes of various kinds, to the amount of about 20 l.;, with various bills of articles bought since the robbery, which I produce—( three of these bills were read, one dated the 15 th of March, for 3 l., one of the 6 th of May, for 1 l.; 17 s. 6 d., and one the 24 th of June, for 1 l.; 15 s. 8 1/2 d.)—the keys of these boxes were fouud in Wigzell's pocket—the goods found corresponded with the bills.
JAMES LEWIS ASHMAN ( police-constable F 119.) I was in company with Pocock in searching the places—I knew Buckley some years ago—I do not know Wigzell.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Where did you know Buckley? A. He kept a public-house on our division seven or eight years ago—I have not seen him for several years.
CHARLES PATRICK CONNELLY re-examined. I think I had seen Wigzell on one or two occasions previous to the robbery—on the day I missed my money I inquired of Brown where Buckley lived—I went, and found her in Wigzell's house, in Cross-court, Drury-lane—I did not go up stairs—Buckley and Wigzell came down stairs together, and spoke to me respecting this affair, saying they were very sorry I had lost so much money—from the exterior appearance, I should say the apartments were rented at 4 s. 6 d. or 5 s. a week.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18440819-2181]
2181. PIETRO VALLER, FRANCISCO ENRICO , and HENRY HARRISON were indicted for feloniously making and engraving certain parts of a foreign undertaking for the payment of money, issued by a body corporate recognised by his Majesty the King of the Netherlands.—Other COUNTS, varying the manner of stating the charge.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
LOUIS GLUCK ROSENTHAL . I am an artist, and live in Wellington-terrace, Waterloo-road—I occupy some rooms on the second floor of the house. On Thursday, the 18th of July, the prisoner Harrison called on me—my landlady came up to me, and then Harrison came up—he said he had heard the report of the work of the Duke of Sussex—I had published a portrait of the late Duke of Sussex—after half an hour's conversation about the Duke of Sussex, he said, "I can give you a job to do of my own"—he said the name of Hatton had introduced me to him—I did not know such a person—I said I should be very glad to do the job if it was in my line—he went down stairs, and said he would bring me an answer in two minutes' time—my curiosity led me to the window to see where he went, and it was into the Waterloo-road, which is just before my door—I saw the other two prisoners communicate with him—he came up stairs in a minute's time, presented to me this paper marked "A," and asked if I could do 350 the same as this genuine one—I said I should think I could do it—he asked how much I wanted to have for 350—I said 3 l.; 10 s.—he said, "I have nothing to do, I must go down stairs, and ask the others"—he went down for two minutes, came back again, and said, "You can make, instead of 350, 500 for the same price"—I agreed to that—he asked me what time he could have the first impression—I said he could have one on Friday, but, being my Sabbath, I asked him to
call on Saturday—I asked him where he resided—he said, "Never mind where I live"—I told him he must leave me a few shillings deposit—he said, "Well, perhaps I will, but I must go down stairs again"—he returned, brought me half-a-sovereign, and said, "I want change, I will leave you 5 s."—I gave him 5 s. out of the half-sovereign—I told him he could have the impression on Saturday—he said, "I will call to-morrow evening or Saturday for the first impression," and he said, "You have no reason for the deposit, because this genuine one which I leave with you is of the value of 2 l.;?"—he told me the 500 must be printed twelve on one sheet, and to avoid the watermark as much as possible—he enjoined me that I should not tell anybody about this job, to keep it a secret—at that time I did not know the value of the instrument, but I had some suspicions—I showed it to a friend—I went to the City to make inquiry, and then went to the police-court, Union-hall; and on the following day, in consequence of what I heard there, I went to the office of the Consul-General of the Netherlands—I saw Mr. Owen, an engraver, on Friday, and engaged him to do the work—on Saturday evening, at a quarter to seven o'clock, he brought me a copper-plate; and while he was with me, I saw, from my window, Enrico and Harrison walking together opposite my house—I pointed them out to Owen, and directed him to go down stairs—about seven o'clock Harrison came to the house—that was the time appointed—he asked me if the first impression was done—I showed him the copper-plate—he asked why it was not done—I told him I could not get the same paper as the original—he said, "Never mind this time about the paper, next time I will get paper from Amsterdam"—he looked at the plate, said nothing about it, but took it down stairs, as he said, to show to the other parties—he said, "I shall not bring it up stairs, I will hand it to you at the shop door"—he brought it back to me, and said, "Correct"—I only saw Enrico with him that day—I told him if he called on Monday evening, at the same time, he should have the first impression—Owen brought me some printed papers on Monday evening—these are the proofs he brought me—Harrison called at seven o'clock on Monday—I showed them to him—he seemed contented with them—he went down stairs to show them to the other party—I did not see anybody outside at that time—I pointed out to him some defects in the engraving, and said the whole must either be cut in wood or stereotyped, to resemble the original—he did not make any observation, but was very agitated that the first impression was not done—I was to go on getting it done in wood—he said he would call on Wednesday evening—there is an ornament on the original document, scrollwork—he said all those spaces must be kept open, so that there should be no kind of join there—it seems not to join in the original—I told him the first impression would be done on Wednesday—he came on Wednesday evening, about the same time—the engraving on wood was not quite ready, but the first impression, the proof from the wood, was done—he told me I ought to have asked the parties five guineas, instead of 3 l.; 10 s.—I said, "Well, I have only seen you, I have nothing to do with the parties"—he said, "Never mind, you might ask the party when he calls to-morrow evening"—he said he would send his card with the party to-morrow evening, that I might see the party, and he said, "You should charge him five guineas, instead of 3 l.; 10 s."—he said, "You must know they have given me 10 s. as a deposit, and I have only given you 5 s., and therefore, when he calls on you to-morrow, you might ask him five guineas, because I want to have a sovereign out of it for my trouble"—I said, "Very well, send me the party"—he said he would call himself the following day, and I should pay him the 15 s. out of the 5 l.; I was to ask the foreigner—he told me not to part with the original until they had paid
me when they came for it—on the Thursday evening I received some more proofs from Owen—these marked "F" and "G" are them—on the same Thursday evening Valler came, and produced an envelope, containing this card, with the name of Harrison on it, but no address—I asked him if he knew German—he said, "No, I know a little French," and asked me if the 500 were ready—I said I could not get them ready, because they were not dry—I said I had received a proof, and I showed him the proof which Owen had brought that evening—he said, "That will do, it is very correct"—I said it was a difficult job, as Harrison had told me to say, and asked him a sovereign more—I could not in my conscience ask him 5 l.;—he said, "Well, you shall have 4 l.;," and gave me a sovereign on account—he took the proof away with him—on the Friday evening Harrison and Valler both came to my room, rather before seven—I told them the copies were not quite ready—part had been printed, but not the whole—I promised him faithfully to have them ready by Saturday evening at that time—Valler was very vexed and discontented that they were not done—I promised them faithfully for the next day, at six o'clock—on Saturday, about five o'clock, I received a quantity of copies, made up into five parcels, in brown paper, from the sergeant of the police—I also received the original coupon, and a block or two—I opened the parcel containing the blocks—these are them—I tied the parcel up again, and about seven o'clock Harrison and Valler came—they asked if the job was done—I delivered to Valler the five parcels I had received from the police, with the original coupon on the top of the others—he opened the parcel, and showed it to Harrison—they looked at the impressions—Harrison made no further observation, Valler, saying that for the future I should have plenty of work to do quite in another style, he seemed perfectly satisfied—Harrison said nothing, but seemed as well pleased—Valler then paid me 2 l. 10 s., the remainder of the money—when I handed the paper containing the blocks to Valler, I said he should take that away with him—he said, "I shall leave it with you"—I said, "No, you had better take it with you," and he took them away, with the 500 impressions—these are them—Valler left the room first, and after he had left, Harrison sat down in a chair, and said, "Now settle with me as well, let me have for my own trouble the money"—I gave him 5 s., and told him he had better call to-morrow for the 5 s.; but he said, "Never mind, pay me now;" and I paid him—I was acting in concert with the police and the Consul all through.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. I suppose you had never seen Valler before? A. No, he was quite a stranger to me—I do not know how the blocks and things came into the hands of the police—I knew it to be wrong—I went on with it, by the direction of the police—I made the extra charge for Harrison's sake—it was not my hand that engraved the plate, it was Owen's—I have had jobs in engraving, but in any honest line—I am an artist, but take any job for a few shillings.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You say on one occasion Harrison was agitated that the first impression was not done, was not what he said, "You give me a great deal of trouble in calling so often, and I shall not get at all more paid for it?" A. I did not hear that—he said, "I only wish you would tell me a certain time, that I am to call, either morning or evening, and I shall have less trouble."
WILLIAM OWEN . I am an engraver, and live in Albemarle-street, Clerkenwell. I got these blocks made for Rosenthal, by people employed by me—he called on me—I went to his house on the Saturday, and took the copper-plate with me—while I was there, he pointed my attention—I looked out of window, and saw Harrison and Enrico on the opposite side, walking past—they
were together—after looking at them, by Rosenthal's direction, I left the room and Went down stairs—I saw Harrison come into the house about ten minutes after I went down, and a minute or two after I saw Harrison in the place—I was in the back parlour—he went up stairs while I was down—Harrison had a small paper parcel at the time he came into the house again—he passed it to Rosenthal, and said it would do very well—that was in the shop—it was the copper-plate which I had brought—I heard him say, "They must be done without fail on the Monday, by seven o'clock in the evening"—I took the copper-plate back with me, and the other part was to be done in wood—I saw no more of them that day—I went to Rosenthal's again on Monday, at a quarter before seven, and took these proofs with me, which are from the letterpress part—while I was there that evening, I noticed Enrico and Harrison outside, and as they passed they met Valler, who joined them—they remained there in my sight about half an hour—I proceeded to engrave the wood blocks, and took proof from them to Rosenthal's on the Thursday—I did not, on that occasion, see anybody near the house—I gave the proofs to Rosenthal—these are them—I was aware, all this time, that Rosenthal was, in communication with the police, and I went to them myself before I commenced.
BETSEY PAVEY . This house in Wellington-terrace, where Rosenthal lives, is my husband's. I remember on Thursday, the 18th of July, Harrison calling, and at the same time I observed Enrico and Valler outside—Harrison came again on the Saturday and on the Monday following—and on the Monday I saw Enrico and Valler outside, while Harrison was there—on the Wednesday following, I remember Harrison coming alone, and next day Valler came himself—the next day Valler and Harrison came together—while they were with Rosenthal I saw Enrico outside, who joined a fourth person—Valler called again on the Saturday, with Harrison, and I saw Enrico outside then—they came three times that Saturday evening, and—I saw Enrico outside on those occasions.
STEPHEN THORNTON ( police-sergeant.) I am one of the detective force. I was employed on this business by the solicitors to the consul-general of the Netherlands. On the Friday I was near the York Hotel, Waterloo-road, about half-past five o'clock, and saw the prisoner Enrico sitting on a cask, inside the house—I was there myself—I did not observe what he was doing—about half-past seven I observed him in company with the other two prisoners and another—they were in conversation together at the corner of York-road, in Waterloo-road, about fifty yards from Rosenthal's house—I followed them down York-road, to the Obelisk—that is going from Rosenthal's—they remained in conversation a short time at the Obelisk, and then separated—as they went down towards the Obelisk, I observed Harrison frequently turning round and looking behind—I was not in my uniform—when they got to the Obelisk, Enrico and Harrison went down the London-road, and Valler and the one not in custody down Blackfriars-road—I followed Valler and the one not in custody over Blackfriars-bridge, up Holborn, down Leather-lane, into a court in Eyre-street, Leather-lane—I did not notice them speak to anybody in the course of their walk—I followed Valler till one o'clock, to the Italian school, in Greville-st., where he remained about an hour—he came out of there with another party—I followed them to Lincoln's Inn-fields; and further on, I saw Valler meet the witness Fielitz in Holborn, and have some conversation with him—I left Valler at last at Eyre-court, Eyre-street-hill—next day, Saturday, I was in the neighbourhood of Waterloo-road, and at half-past six saw the three prisoners—Valler and Harrison went to Rosenthal's door and rang the bell, and two, I believe it to be Harrison and Valler, went in—Enrico remained outside—I did not set them come
out, but I saw them after they had come out, and saw them all three together—Valler was carrying a small parcel, wrapped in this handkerchief-ther joined Enrico almost immediately they came out of Rosenthal's—I followed them to the Hero of Waterloo, in Waterloo-road—I went into the house—the three prisoners were sitting in a box by themselves in the parlour—Valler was sitting outside the box—the papers all laid between Enrico and Valler—I said to Valler, "You are my prisoner;" and in the act of my reaching for the parcel, Valler put his hand out to catch the parcel—I caught it in his hand—they were all three taken into custody, and taken to the station—I searched Valler, and found on him a pocket-book, and the blocks and a paper in his left-hand coat pocket—the parcel was fire separate parcels of the impressions—I found this proof "F," among other things, in his pocket-book, and also this ( the genuine coupon, marked "A")—I also found these papers on him—("K," "L," "M," and "N,")—I did not search Enrico.
DAVID GALLICO . I am an Italian, and am an interpreter, living in Litchfield-street, Soho. I know Valler and Enrico—I knew Valler by the name of Giacome San Michele, and Enrico by the name of Francois Mutta—on Monday, the 22nd of July, I saw Enrico by the Duke of York column, at the bottom of Waterloo-place, between five and a quarter after—Fielitz, a German, was with me—we had some conversation together, and I left Fielitz, and joined Enrico—we afterwards entered a public-house, and had a glass of cider, and after a bit he said to me, "I am obliged to leave you, as I have an appointment on the other side the Waterloo-road, at seven o'clock exactly"—I walked with him as far as the Strand, near Waterloo-bridge, and then left him—Valler saw me six or seven weeks ago as I was on Saffron-hill—he called me aside privately, and said he wished to speak to me, he had got a job for me—I asked him what it was—he said, to do some writing, some language for him—he appointed to call at my house—I gave him my direction—I said if I could not do it most likely my wife could, as she writes well—I told him so—he did not come—I met him afterwards, and he afterwards came in about a week—I wrote my address for him, and he called, and said he was very sorry the writing would not do—( looking at the paper marked "L")—this is my address in my handwriting, which I gave him—I afterwards got my wife to write, and handed it to Valler—this is it (M)—he said that most likely would do—he called about a week afterwards at my place early in the morning, and said he was very sorry I could not suit the writing, but he should be glad if I could recommend him anybody to write, and I introduced Fielitz to him on Friday, the 26th of last month, at a public-house in Bear-street, Leicester-square—we had some gin to drink—Valler on that occasion produced this passport, and desired Fielitz to imitate the signature of C. Salati on that—Fielitz tried to imitate it—Valler said he was quite satisfied, and what he would give him to do, the signature he wanted him to do would not be so long as Salati—he then produced this (F), and said that was the work, and he wished him to put a full name similar to that, and he should have fifty for him—he appointed next Saturday to call at the house at eight in the morning to come to my residence—he never came.
ADOLPHE FIELITZ . I am a Prussian, and an interpreter by profession; I live in Cecil-court, Leicester-square. I know Gallico—he introduced me to Valler, who told me he had something to write—he showed me a passport and spoke to me about imitating that signature—he asked me if I could write the same as that—I said, yes I could—I wrote just the same, and he was satisfied, and said, "That will do"—some time afterwards he took out his pocket-book, and showed me some coupons—this is one—he said he wanted the name of the clerk of the banking-house to be written on it, he should want forty-five or fifty done, and said there would be 100 or 200 to do—I met him in
Holborn at eleven o'clock on Friday evening—I did not know him—he came up to me, and shook hands—next day I went to the office of the Consul of the Netherlands, and gave information.
EDWARD KENDALL ( police-sergeant.) I accompanied Thornton on Friday, the 26th, to the York-hotel, Waterloo-road, and saw Enrico sitting on the end of a cask looking out of window—he could command a view of the road—Thornton and I separated—I went a short distance down the road, and saw Enrico come out, and stand at the door—he remained there about a quarter of an hour, and was joined by Harrison—Harrison went to Rosenthal's—I at last joined in taking them—I searched Enrico and Harrison—on Enrico I found a pocket-book containing two or three cards with an inscription on them—he gave his address, No. 4, Trafalgar-place, Gibraltar-row, Lambeth—I went to that house, and went all over it—there are two rooms down stairs, and one up—I found nothing but two or three letters.
RUDOLF ADRIAN MEES . I reside at Rotterdam—I am one of the firm of the Administration-office of Jan Van der Hoop and Gerardus Eck. We issue certificates of Dutch stock, accompanied by coupons—this is a genuine coupon issued by our house by authority of the Government of the Netherlands.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Is there any authority in writing? A. It is done under the law—it is in the law of the country—we have no written direction by the law of Holland.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Do you issue them? A. Yes—they are circulated among the population, and are taken just as bank notes—this is worth almost 2 l.; in English money—it is signed "Adrianus Offers"—he is one of the firm there are six members of the firm—Jan Van der Hoop is one of them—on presenting this coupon at our office when due the person would be entitled to that amount of money—we receive a general sum from the Government of the Netherlands to pay to the holders of the coupons—William II. is the reigning King of the Netherlands.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Has he any other name but William? A. Not to my knowledge.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. You are in partnership with these gentlemen? A. We are a commercial society, and sue and are sued in the name of the Administration-office—that is the title—we are a partnership and act as agents to the Government under the law—this is a perfect paper—it has no signature or water mark—every coupon has a number corresponding with the certificate it belongs to—we are sued as the Administration-office of Jan Van der Hoop and others.
VALLER — GUILTY . Aged 23.
ENRICO — GUILTY . Aged 52.
HARRISON — GUILTY . Aged 45.
Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
