2182. AGNES FITZGERALD was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Bridget Slattery, about the hour of five in the night of the 20th of May, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, I cloak, value 10 s.; 1 pillow, 3 s.; 3 handkerchiefs,. 4 s. 6 d.; 2 aprons, 1 s.; 1 cap, 6 d.; and 1 bonnet, 1 s.; her goods.
BRIDGET SLATTERY . I am single, and live in Nelson-street, Camberwell. On Sunday the 18th of May, the prisoner came to my house—I had known her many years, but had not seen her for sixteen years—she slept at my house that night, and I gave her a breakfast in the morning—about a quarter to six o'clock on Tuesday morning I found her in my room—she awoke me out of my sleep—she must have got in through the window—I asked how she
got in, and she said at the window—she laid down in the wash-house—I gave her her breakfast—I did not see her again till she was in custody—I afterwards missed the articles stated, which are worth 10 s. altogether—I have seen the cloak and apron since—I looked at the clock when I found her—it was a quarter before six.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Is yours a Dutch clock? A. I do not know.
JOHANNA HEFFERAM . The prisoner lodged in my house two nights—she had no money to pay, and left me a duplicate for a cloak—I gave it to the policeman, and pointed out to him the room she occupied.
WILLIAM BAKER ( police-constable E 108.) I received a duplicate from Hefferam, and searched the room she pointed out as the prisoner's—I found an apron—I went to Mr. Benton's, the pawnbroker's, in Tottenham-court-road, and got this cloak with the duplicate.
MARY ANN CALLAGAN . I lived at Hefferam's house—the prisoner gave me this apron the first night she came there, the night before she pawned the cloak.
MRS. SLATTERY re-examined. This is my apron and cloak.
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 47.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Justice Wightman.
[trialAccount t18440819-2183]
2183. JOHN SULLIVAN, alias Michael Connell , was indicted for uttering a counterfeit sixpence to Ann Moore, having been previously convicted of a like offence.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am assistant solicitor to the Mint. I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of John Sullivan, with another, in this Court, in Dec. 1842—I have examined it with the original in this office—it is a true copy—( read.)
JOHN EATON . I am a plumber and glazier—I was present in Dec. 1842, when the prisoner was tried for uttering counterfeit coin.
EDWARD HILLIER . My brother-in-law keeps the Monument public-house, Union-street, St. Saviour's. On the 29th of June, about ten o'clock in the evening, I was attending there at the bar—the prisoner called for a pint of beer, which came to 2 d.—he handed me a 6 d.—I gave him the change and put the 6 d. into the till—there was no other 6 d. there—he drank the beer and went away—he came again within ten minutes with a female, called for a glass of gin, to the best of my recollection, and paid 6 d. for it—I gave him change, and put that 6 d. into the till—after the gin was drunk he and the woman went away—soon after Ann Moore went to the till in my presence—no other 6 d. had been put in there—she discovered that the sixpences were bad—we looked at them and then threw them to the back of the till by themselves—some time after I saw her take these two sixpences from the till—I marked, and delivered them to the policeman.
Prisoner. Q. Did not Mrs. Moore take one? A. I took two, but there were three—I did not say at the office that I never found these out till I was giving change for a sovereign—we had not change for a sovereign in the till.
ANN MOORE . I am house-keeper to Daniel Aymer, who keeps the Monument public-house. On Saturday the 19th of June I went to the till—I found two bad sixpences—there were no other sixpences there—in a few minutes afterwards I saw the prisoner again, and a female with him they asked for a pint of porter first, and tendered 2 d. in payment—after that they had half a quartern of gin—it came to 2 d.—the prisoner gave me a bad 6 d.—I detained it—I did not give him the change—I put it at the back of the
till, separate from the other two—the prisoner was shortly afterwards apprehended—the sixpences were given to the policeman.
Prisoner. Q. You said before you gave me the third back? A. No—I did not give any sixpence back.
JOHN BIDGOOD . I keep the George, in Great Suffolk-street, not quite half a mile from Mr. Aymer's. On the night of the 29th of June I saw the prisoner at my house—he was alone—he called for a glass of ale—I served him—it came to 1 d.—he gave me a sixpence—I took it and bent it on the counter, took a knife, cut it in two, and threw it out of doors—I followed him to the door, and saw two women waiting for him, one of whom I recognised as having tendered me a bad piece on the Thursday morning—I gave him into custody—I did not preserve the pieces I had broken—I am sure it was bad—I could not have bent a good one.
JOSEPH TAYLOR ( police-constable M 156.) On Saturday night, the 29th of Jane, I received the prisoner into custody from Mr. Bidgood—I searched him, and found 11 d. in copper on him.
Prisoner. It was 1 s. in copper.
RICHARD WHEATLEY ( police-constable M 161.) I received three sixpences from Ann Moore, which I produce, and the other two from Hillier.
GEORGE HALLETT . I am turnkey at the county gaol, Newington. On Tuesday, the 2nd of July, the prisoner was passed into my custody—I searched him, and found three counterfeit sixpences in his right-hand trowsers pocket—he made a grab at them, and said, "Give me that, "or words to that effect—I secured them—he said, "Now you can't hang me."
Prisoner. When I came into the prison I was searched in the presence of the governor, and nothing was found on me; when I was searched in the clerk's presence by him and the turnkey, my pockets were turned inside out, and I had nothing; I went into the yard, and got my dinner; this man called me, and said, "I want you;" he came and put his hand so, and said, "I have got you now."
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. This sixpence, uttered to Ann Moore, is counterfeit—the other two, uttered to Hillier, are also counterfeit; also the three found by Hallett.
GUILTY . Aged 51.— Transported for Ten Years.

[trialAccount t18440819-2186]
2186. THOMAS FITZGERALD and THOMAS COOMBS were indicted for stealing 1 till, value 6 d.; 1 shilling, 4 sixpences, 1 groat, 30 pence, 76 halfpence, and 48 farthings; the property of Elizabeth Gadd; and that Fitzgerald had been before convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH GADD . I am a widow, and keep a general shop in Regent-street, Lambeth. On the 6th of July, about half-past twelve o'clock, Annetts came in and told me something, and I missed my till from under my counter, where I had seen it safe about half an hour before—it had about 10 s. in it, in silver and copper—there were some farthings, halfpence, pence, and one or two shillings—this is my till.
EDWIN JAMES ANNETTS . I live in Caroline-street, Lambeth-walk. About
twelve o'clock on the 6th of July I was in Lambeth-walk—I saw the prisoners together—they turned down Regent-street—Fitzgerald went into one shop previous to his going to Mrs. Gadd's—he came out again, and they went down Regent-street—I saw Coombs standing out by a post—Fitzgerald went into Gadd's shop, and came out with the till in his two hands—they then both turned round the corner, and were out of sight—I have been in the police, and had seen both the prisoners before, and knew them—I pursued them at fast as I could—they ran down Dorie-street—there is a kind of bend in that street, and I lost sight of them, but just as I got near the corner I heard the sound of money rattling—I got round the corner, and saw these two farthings—the prisoner had got rid of the till, and had something in the centre of a handkerchief, and both of them had hold of it—I called, "Stop thief," but I could not catch them—I went to a garden at the corner of Dorie-street, and the persons said they had picked up the till—I got this till there—the prisoners had passed that place as they were going—I am superannuated through injuries I received in the force.
Fitzgerald. Q. Where were you when I brought out the till? A. Some distance up Regent-street—I am positive of you, because I had seen you numbers of times, and I saw you come down the street.
Coombs. Q. How far were you off when I was by the post? A. About thirty yards—I did not say at Union-hall that I was 200 yards off—I knew you.
Fitzgerald's Defence. I was at home at the time of the robbery; I stopped in my own neighbourhood for three weeks after, before I was taken; this man is taking a false oath.
Coombs's Defence. I can prove that I was at Chatham, selling skewen and brushes; I do not know this lad; I never saw him in my life.
WILLIAM COMPTON ( police-constable P 159.) I produce a certificate of Fitzgerald's former conviction, which 1 got at the Clerk of the Peace for Surrey—( read)—he is the person.
FITZGERALD — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
COOMBS — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2188. JOSEPH MARKS was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM HARDY . I am a manufacturer of orchill, and live in Little George-street, Bermondsey—the prisoner was in my service, and when he received money he was to pay it to Mr. Bailey if I was not at home, and if Mr. Bailey was from home to Mr. Brookman—he never paid this 4 s. to me—I requested him to put down on a slate what he received.
JAMES MURRELL . I am a leather-dresser, and live in Kent-street. I paid the prisoner 7 s. 11 d. for his master on the 14th of July, for some orchill.
WILLIAM BROOKMAN . I received from the prisoner 3 s. 11 d., for Mr. Hardy—I am sure I received no more.
ALEXANDER HOBSON ( police-constable M 221.) I took the prisoner—he said Mr. Murrell's man told him to send 14lbs. of stuff, and put 14lbs. of water to it, and they were to share the money.
Prisoner's Defence. I received 7 s. 11 d.; I paid 3 s. 11 d. to Mr. Hardy's man, and 4 s. Mr. Murrell's man had.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18440819-2189]
2189. MARY DONOVAN was indicted for stealing 1 table-cloth, value 1 s.; 3 shifts, 4 s.; 1 towel, 9 d.; 3 night-gowns, 2 s. 6 d.; and 2 pinafores, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Henry Mullett, her master.
SARAH MULLETT . I am the wife of Henry Mullett. The prisoner was my servant—she left me—I did not miss anything of consequence when she left—the articles stated were taken from the laundress's after she was dismissed—I did not authorise her to take them.
MARY ANN STONE . I mangle for Mrs. Mullett. On the 6th of April the prisoner came and said she was come for her mistress's mangling, which was three shifts, a table-cloth, and the other things—believing her to be in Mrs. Mullett's service I gave her the things.
FREDERICK WOODTHORP . I am in the service of Mr. Bradley, a pawnbroker—these shifts and other things were brought on the 6th of April, by a female who gave her name as Ann Muller.
Prisoner. My mistress gave me the money, and sent me for the clothes; I gave them to Mary, who is in her service now; you know you gave me the money, and I gave the things to Mary.
MRS. MULLETT. No, I did not—it is no such thing—these are my articles—I have the little girl now that I had then—she is not here—the prisoner did not tell the Magistrate that I gave her the money—she said she had never had the things.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18440819-2190]
2190. JAMES MACDONALD was indicted for embezzlement.
SAMUEL ALLARDICE . I am a baker, and live in the Borough-market. The prisoner was in my service—it was his duty to carry bread to my customers, and if he received money he was to pay it to me on the same day—he has never paid me either of the sums in question, I am sure.
HENRY SKINNER . I deal with Mr. Allardice. I paid the prisoner 2 s. 11 1/2 d. for his master, on the 10th of June.
WILLIAM FITCH . I paid the prisoner 4 s. 1 d. on the 27th of May, for his master.
Prisoner's Defence. While in the prosecutor's employ I served many publicans with bread, and that caused me to take more liquor than I ought to have done. My master was in the habit of taking all the money out of my pocket, and detaining it till he thought proper to balance; be then used to ask if I knew what money I had, and if there has been a deficiency I have made it good. To the best of my knowledge my master has received these monies, for I never kept any book or tally.
SAMUED ALLARDICE re-examined. He came home intoxicated one evening in July, after he had received a bill of 2 l.; 6 s. 5 d.; I made the men search him, and he had 2 l.; 0 s. 7 1/2 d. in his pocket. I had not found out then that he had received these two sums, and did not ask him for them—he never paid them to me.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18440819-2191]
2191. WILLIAM DAWSON and CHARLES HENDERSON were indicted for stealing 14 spoons, value 5 l.; 5 s.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, 8 s.; and 3 forks, 15 s.; the goods of Robert White, in his dwelling-house; to which
DAWSON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
CAROLINE BOLDING . I am servant to Mr. Robert White—he lives in Warwick-place, Peckham-rye, in the parish of St. Giles, Camberwell—it is his dwelling-house. On the 27th of July I brought the plate into the kitchen to clean—I then went up stairs, and there was an alarm that some one had
got in at the kitchen-window—I came down, and the plate was nearly all gone—the plate produced is part of it, and is my master's.
HENRY BRIDGE . I was in my garden, and heard the cry of "Stop thief", I saw the two prisoners running in a direction from the prosecutor's house, in a private road, and, whilst they were running, Dawson threw a yellow parcel away—after the prisoners were taken I went back to see what the parcel was—I was looking about for it for some time, and Henderson said, "You b—y snot what are you looking for?"—I found three pieces of leather, and these spoons, and other plate, in the hedge—I took it up to Mr. White's.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What are you? A. I am out of business, and have been a publican—when Henderson spoke to me, he and Dawson had been brought back in custody of a brick-maker to where they had thrown the plate away—I gave them into the custody of a policeman at Peckham—they were stopped about forty yards from where I found the yellow parcel—I was in my garden when I first heard the cry, and got over the hurdles.
JAMES BANK . I live in North-street, Lock's-fields. I saw the prisoners together against Mr. White's garden-gate, and when I got round the water, which is opposite the door, I saw Dawson go in at the window, and come out—Henderson was standing outside the gate, and could see if anybody came—they then went away together.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you a dealer in hearth-stones? A. Yes—the garden is not very large—I saw Dawson go up to the house.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
HENDERSON — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18440819-2192]
2192. JOSEPH SINGLETON was indicted for stealing 1 gelding price 5 l.;, the property of James Elliott.
JAMES HOLMES . I am a carman, and live in Camden-street, Newington. The prisoner came to me on the 18th of July—he said he wanted a horse to draw a carriage from Parliament-street to Mr. Herriot's—my man harnessed the horse, and went through to get the whip, and when he came back the prisoner and the horse were gone—I had not let it to the prisoner, because the man went through to get the whip—it was about twelve o'clock in the day, and at three the next day I found the horse in Smithfield—it was a gelding, and was the property of Mr. James Elliott—he sent it to my stable, as he had no work for it—the prisoner might have had it if he had asked for it—he said he was to have 6 s. for the job—he was to have 2 s. of it, and 14 s.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Was it a man of the name of Lane the prisoner was to have it for? A. No, he did not mention the name of Lane as the person, to my recollection—I did not put him on the horse myself—I said he might do without a whip.
GEORGE BLUNT . I am a greengrocer, and live in Hill-place, Ray-street. On the 18th of July I bought a gelding of the prisoner, about four or five o'clock in the evening—he said he was going to take it to be slaughtered—he asked me 34 s. for it—I gave him 29 s.—I took it to Smithfield the next day.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you buy this? A. In the street, and paid for it in the Spencer's Arms, a public-house opposite Hicks's Hall, which we went over to—there was only the landlord, the prisoner, and I, and another man who did not interfere—I had never seen the prisoner before, to my knowledge—he was with me half an hour—I had half a pint of cider, and he had half a pint of beer—we met in the public-house—I wanted a horse, and he said he wanted a better one, and he would buy one on the Friday—this
horse was poor—I did not sell it—Mr. Holmes came and said, "This is my horse"—he got the policeman, and I went to Union Hall—the prisoner was not in custody then—I saw him about a fortnight afterwards at Union Hall—he came soon after I got there—I saw him put into the dock—I did not say I thought he was not the man—I said, "That is the man"—I am sure he is the man—I cannot swear to the time exactly—it was between four and five in the afternoon, as near as I can recollect.
JOHN BRANN . I am a policeman of Maidstone. The prisoner, whom I had seen once before, came to me on the 22nd of July, and said he wished to give himself up for horse stealing, he and another had stolen a horse.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he drunk? A. No, sober—he said, "I want to give myself into your custody"—I said, "For what?"—he said, "Horse steling"—I said, "From whom did you steal the horse?"—he said, "From Mr. Holmes, at Walworth"—he said there was a previous charge, and I should find that on him—I took him to the station, and found a bill on him that he was out on bail—he did not mention the name of the other man—I cautioned him, and asked him what reason he had for giving himself up—he said through the men that were bail for him.
MR. CHAKNOCK called
HENRY NEVILL . I am a coal-porter. The prisoner used to work as a coal-heaver—on Thursday, the 18th of July, I was with him at the Waterman's Arms, Belvidere-road—my brother was there, but he is now gone into the country—I was with the prisoner there from one o'clock till nearly four—I and he were working that day for Messrs. Cole and Child—I know what day it was, because it was the first day I had worked for them for these two years—I know I was with him till four o'clock, because I had then to go to work—there was a clock in the yard—I cannot tell how far that is from Silver-street, Clerkenwell—I should think it is a mile and a half or two miles—I did not leave the prisoner at the public-house—he went away, and told me he was going home—I am certain I was with him from one o'clock till four.
COURT. Q. What work had you to do that day? A. To load the wagon—we do it at so much per ton—I began work that day at six o'clock in the morning, and worked till six at night, but from one till four there were no wagons to load—my brother was not at work with me, but he could prove the prisoner was there from one till four o'clock that day.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18440819-2195]
2195. WILLIAM EDWARD BROWN was indicted for embezzling I shilling, the monies of James Dobson, his master.
JAMES DOBSON . I am a laceman, and live in High-street, Borough. In consequence of some circumstances I made an arrangement with Mrs. Bell, on the evening of the 26th of July—I marked 25 s., and the following morning, at ten o'clock, Mrs. Bell was to spend the whole or part of it—she came, and
spent 24 s.—shortly after I met Mrs. Bell I returned to my house, and sent the prisoner out for an errand—I threw a shilling on the counter from my pocket, but I drew the till out and shut it again, as if I took the shilling from the till—he went out, and staid two or three minutes—I then examined the till, and missed one shilling, but I thought he might have spent it, and I did not take him then—the till owed him 6 d. for some coppers that he had put is in the morning, and in an hour and a half my wife passed half-a-crown into his hand, and he gave her two shillings, which she immediately passed into my hand, and one of them was the marked shilling which was missing from the drawer—he did not take them from the drawer, but from his waistcoat pocket—my wife was not aware of the marks on the money—I was fearful to give the prisoner into custody—I consulted the officers, and I directed them to come at six o'clock in the evening, and take him—they found another marked shilling on him which was not missing at the time the other was.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long had he been in your employ? A. About six or seven weeks—I had been applied to by a gentleman to get him a shopman—the prisoner would not do for a shop that was returning 40, 000 l.; a year, but he might do for me, who was not doing one-tenth of the business—I wrote to my friend that he was of no use at all, and then took him myself two days afterwards—I let him go, and then took him again—I had a fire at my house from four to six weeks ago—it was perhaps ten days or a fortnight before the prisoner was taken up—I employed him to assist me in making out a list of the articles that were destroyed.
Q. From that time up to the time when you gave him into custody, had you continually employed a watch to see if you could find him tripping in any way? A. An errand boy in my service had received orders to keep his eye on the goods both before and after the fire—the errand boy is not here—I took him to Union Hall, and Mr. Traill requested he might not be sworn—I had employed my wife to watch the prisoner—it was about half-past ten in the morning when I first discovered that my money was short, and I gave him in charge at six in the evening—the policemen were to attend at six o'clock—I went to the police immediately after the first shilling was received from the prisoner, which was missed from the till, the one my wife received from him on giving him half-a-crown—that was at twelve o'clock in the day, and I went to the police between twelve and one, desiring them to wait for further information—I never charged my wife with stealing anything from me, and never said so—she has left me in consequence of my conduct—I never brought her and her children home naked in their night clothes in the middle of the night—I believe them to be dressed—I did not examine their clothes—I waited a sufficient time for them to be dressed—I had not been to the drawer myself, and taken out a shilling before I charged the prisoner with any offence to the police—I did not go to the drawer, and take out a shilling, and give it to the prisoner to buy stay-laces, and I never said so in the hearing of Mr. or Mrs. Bell—I gave him a shilling, but I took it out of my right hand pocket-—I went to the till, and drew it out, and put it back again—I threw a shilling on the counter to lead him to suppose that I had been to the till for it—it is our custom to pass money on the counter—when Mrs. Bell left the shop in the morning there was a customer who demanded the price of a collar in the shop—that collar was missing from the window, and there was not a shilling for it—I did not ask Miss Millen to give me a note to engage that the prisoner should not bring an action against me.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440819-2196]
2196. WILLIAM EDWARD BROWN was again indicted for stealing 1 shilling, the monies of James Dobson, his master.
JAMES DOBSON . I missed one of the marked shillings, in the till, and the policeman found a marked shilling on the prisoner, at six o'clock in the evening—I know nothing further—we did not count the money in the till all day—if people came and wanted change, the money was used—there were 24 s. in the till in the morning—we did not count it again in the evening—the money was paid out in the day, and it might be brought back again—I was advised, at the station, to give the prisoner into custody—that was the reason why I did not charge him at the moment—one marked shilling was found on him in the evening, which was one I had given to Mrs. Bell—silver had gone out, perhaps twenty times in the day, in the course of business—we do not keep check-books—Mrs. Bell was not before the Magistrate—she was unwell at the time, and she was unwilling to attend.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Upon your oath, did you not say to the prisoner that you had kept Mrs. Bell back? A. No.
KATE PANCHIN BELL . Mr. Dobson brought me some money, which he told me was marked—I was to lay it out at his shop—I laid out 24 s. about ten o'clock in the morning.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know the prisoner? A. No—he is not an acquaintance of mine—I was not well at the prisoner's first examination—I do not know why Mr. Dobson did not produce me at the second examination—when I received this money of Mr. Dobson, I left it where anybody might have access to it—I and my husband understood Mr. Dobson to say that he went round to the till and took out a shilling to give to the prisoner to get some laces.
ELIZABETH DOBSON . I do not know anything about the shilling that was found in the prisoner's pocket.
Cross-examined. Q. You and your husband have had some misunderstandings and quarrels? A. Sometimes we have—he is not more violent than any other gentleman, I believe—he never charged me with having taken anything—I left him, and went to Hampstead, because I wanted to go into the country a little while—he did not bring us back undressed in the middle of the night—we were dressed, except the infant, who was undressed, as infants are.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440819-2197]
2197. GEORGE GRAY was indicted for stealing, at Lambeth, 2 coats, value 25 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, 10 s.; 2 waistcoats, 1 l.; 1 pair of boots, 4 s.; 1 handkerchief, 2 s.; 3 shirts, 10 s.; 1 watch, 1 l.; 1 half-sovereign, 1 sixpence, and 6 halfpence, the property of Richard Kingsmill Greenfield: and 1 waistcoat, 10 s., the goods of William Hole, in his dwelling-house; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
RICHARD KINGSMILL GREENFIELD . I am a carpenter, and live at Greenwich. I was at work at Mr. Hole's, in Waterloo-place, Surrey. On the 14th of March I got up about five o'clock in the morning, and left the prisoner, who was a lodger, in bed—I left all the articles stated, in a box in the room—I returned at eight o'clock in the evening—they were all gone, and my watch and money—the prisoner had left an old coat, a pair of trowsers, and a waistcoat, and had taken mine—he promised to come back that evening to pay his lodging, but he did not come.
Prisoner. Q. When did you miss your things? A. When I came home—your things were destroyed about a week before you were taken.
WILLIAM HOLE . I lived with my father, where the prosecutor lodged—I
saw the prisoner go oat on the 14th of March with a bundle tied up in a blue striped handkerchief.
Prisoner. Q. Where were you when I went out? A. In the front parlour—I heard you tell my mother you would pay her for the lodging when you came home in the evening—she opened the door for you—I saw you through the parlour door.
ELIZABETH HOLE . I am the wife of William Hole. This is our dwelling-house, and is in the parish of Lambeth—I had a man and his wife lodging with me, but no one went into these young men's room, but myself—the prosecutor's secutor's box stood there—I did not see the prisoner go out that morning—he knocked at my door, and said he would come back in the evening, and pay his lodging—he did not come back, nor pay—I lost my husband's waistcoat.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me with a bundle? A. No—I opened the door, but did not see the bundle.
WILLIAM DUBOIS ( police-sergeant N 14.) I took the prisoner—he said he had not lodged there, and did not know such a person.
Prisoner's Defence. If the boy could see me when he was behind the door where the bed is, does it stand to reason that she could not see me with a bundle; I never had any.
WILLIAM GOODLAND . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18440819-2198]
2198. ELIZABETH CRUMP was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 2 s.; 4 sovereigns, 4 half-crowns, 8 shillings, and 4 pence, the property of John James Savidge.
JOHN JAMES SAVIDGE . I am a window-blind maker. On the 15th of July, between twelve and one o'clock, I went to a house in Union-street, Southwark, for a lodging—I paid 1 s., and went to bed by myself—I wrapped my trowsers up, and put them between the mattress and bedstead, became there was no lock to the door—I awoke about four o'clock in the morning and saw the prisoner in my room—I said, "What are you doing here?"—she said, "I am only looking for my comb, Sir"—she said, "If it annoys you I can walk out," and she went out—I awoke again about a quarter to six o'clock—I turned the mattress down to look for my trowsers, and they were gone—I looked under the bed, and there they were—my purse was lying on my trowsers, and the money was gone—I was very tired but not drunk—I live in Fleet-lane, but I had forgotten to take my key when I went out in the morning to spend the day with a friend at Walworth, and I did not like to go to my own house to alarm my lodgers—but the reason I came to go to this place was there was a female crying, and she said, "Pray can you oblige me with a few halfpence, I have three children in Norwood workhouse"—I said, "I have no objection if you can tell me of a decent lodging," and she directed me to that house—I lost a handkerchief, as well as the money—this is the handkerchief—there is a small hole in it, which I know it by.
GEORGE PATTERSON . I am a seaman, and lodge at the house the prosecutor went to—it is a brothel—the prisoner lodged there, and used to sleep in the room that the prosecutor had for that night—she came home at three or four o'clock in the morning—I was in bed—she had her supper in my room, and was told she could not go into her own room—she sat a little while, and then took the candle and went up stairs into the front room—I heard the same footstep come out of the front room, cross the passage, and go
into the back bed-room where the prosecutor was—she then came down into my room, took her bonnet, and went out.
JAMS CURRY ( police-constable L 20.) I took the prisoner riding in a cabin the London-road on the 20th of July—I did not then understand about the robbery, but before the Magistrate I heard the prosecutor had lost a cambric handkerchief—I then went back to the prisoner, who was locked up, asked her for the handkerchief, and she gave it me—she said some girl gave it to her—the prosecutor said it had a small hole, and some piping round it, which it has.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw my prosecutor; it is a false enarge against me, because I was anxious to quit my landlady and return to my trade.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18440819-2204]
2204. PHINEAS TWINE was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM HUBE . I am a newsvender, and live in Surrey-place, Wandsworth. The prisoner was in my service about six months, to distribute newspapers, and receive money from customers occasionally, which he was to pay to me the same evening—on the 22nd of July he ought to have come at six o'clock—he did not come till two, and then I discharged him—I supply Mr. Feathers of Lansdown-road, and Mr. Hobson of the Quadrant, at Wandsworth, with papers—I did not receive any money of the prisoner for Mr. Feathers on the 26th of June—he always kept his money till it was finally settled in this book, and then he paid it over to me—that his money might be safe I gave him this little box to keep it in—he did not bring me any money on the 3rd of July, or on the 10th of July, from Mr. Feathers—I asked if he had been paid, and he said no, Mr. Feathers was out of town—he did not pay me any money from Mr. Hobson on the 20th of July—I asked him about it expressly.
Cross-examined by MR. HORNE. Q. Are you in a large way of business? A. I serve about two hundred Sunday papers, and I may serve seventy daily papers—Mr. Hobson's account was for the Saturday edition of the "Weekly Messenger," and of four days of the "Times"—sometimes he had the "Times" three or four days running, and sometimes only one day—he always paid on Saturday—the prisoner was the only boy who took out papers for me in the week days—on Sunday I have another boy who takes one district, and the prisoner the other—he received money from some customers on Saturday, and from others on Monday and on other days—Mr. Feathers and Mr. Hobson were particularly regular in their payments.
SUSAN SOUTER . I am in the service of Mr. Feathers, of Stockwell. He has newspapers of Mr. Hube—the prisoner brought the papers every morning—on the 26th of June I paid him 1 s. for Mr. Hube, for newspapers which he had supplied; and 1 s. on the 3rd of July, 1 s. on the 10th of July, and 1 s. on the 17th of July.
Cross-examined. Q. What makes you recollect these four particular days? A. I always paid him on Wednesday.
ANN FRANCIS . I am servant to Mr. Hobson, of Wandsworth. I paid the prisoner 1 s. 2 d. for his master—it was Mr. Hobson's money.
Cross-examined. Q. What day was it? A. On the 20th of July, on a Saturday.
JOHN MENNIE ( police-constable V 70.) I took the prisoner on the 5th of Aug., at Clapham—I said it was for embezzling his master's money—he said he was going to the station to give himself up.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 14.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury.— Confined Fourteen Days.

[trialAccount t18440819-2210]
2210. JANE WESTON was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH ASTON . I am servant to Frederick Wood, of Cornwall-road, Surrey. On the 1st of Aug. the prisoner came for half a sheet of writing-paper—I served her, and she gave me a sixpence—I said, "I must get change," which I did, and after she was gone I put the sixpence into my mouth, and found it was bad, because my teeth made a mark in it—I gave the same sixpence to my master.
FREDERICK WOOD . I am the master of Aston. On the 1st of Aug. I received a sixpence from her—I found it was bad—I bent it, and placed it on a shelf where there was no other money, till the Wednesday, when another person brought another bad sixpence—I then took it off the shelf, and gave it to Garforth.
ELIZABETH HOARE . I keep a lodging-house in Roberts'-buildings, Waterloo-road. On the 2nd of Aug., about ten o'clock at night, the prisoner
came to my house for a lodging—I was to charge her 1 s.—she gave me a crown-piece, and I gave her 4 s. change—she went away, to get her supper, as she said, and that she would return—I waited for her—I locked the crown. piece in a box—there was no other there—I looked at it the next morning as she had not come back, and I found it was bad—I gave it to the officer.
THOMAS GARFORTH ( police-constable L 151.) I produce the crown-piece which I got from Mrs. Hoare, and the sixpence which I got from Mr. Wood.
Prisoner, I saw the officer whisper to Aston before she came in; he said, "Are you sure that is the young woman?" and she said, "I think that is her."
ELIZABETH ASTON re-examined. The officer did not say anything to me on the subject of this charge—I am sure the prisoner is the person.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint—these are both counterfeit.
Prisoner's Defence. I gave the 5 s.-piece, but I did not know it was bad; I had it from a gentleman; I know nothing about the sixpence; I should have gone back to the lodging-house, but I met a young woman, who prevented me.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18440819-2211]
2211. JOSEPH WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of John Phipps from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN PHIPPS . I live in Dorrington-grove, Kennington, and am a linen-draper. On the 31 s. t of July, between nine and ten o'clock at night, I was crossing Westminster-bridge—I felt a tug at my pocket, and missed my handkerchief—I looked round—the prisoner and another young man were close by me—the prisoner was moving away from my side—I followed him, and in going across the bridge I saw him putting his hand into his breast pocket—I went up to him and said, "You have got my handkerchief"—he said, "Yes, sir, I have just picked it up"—I said, "I should rather say, picked it out"—I followed him, and gave him in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How far off were you? A. When I felt the twitch I was on the pavement, and he was immediately on the curb—he was not looking at the handkerchief when I first saw him—when I first saw the handkerchief it must have been partly in his hand and partly in mine, as it was taken from his pocket—I know it by its general appearance, and swear to it—I had used it about ten minutes before.
THOMAS BROWN . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined One Year.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.

2213. RICHARD MULLETT was indicted for stealing 1 mare, price 10 l.; 1 chaise, value 10 l.; and 1 set of harness, 4 l.;: the property of Edward Leech Davis.
EDWARD LEECH DAVIS . I keep a livery-stable in White-street, Wellington-street, Blackfriars-road. On the 7th of Aug. the prisoner came, from five to half-past five o'clock in the afternoon—he asked what I should charge for a horse and chaise to go to Croydon—I said 12 s.—he said it was to return
at ten o'clock the same evening—I lent him the horse and chaise—he paid me the 12 s., and left his address—"Charles Webb, 18, Nelson-square"—he did not return that evening, and the next morning I went to No. 18, Nelson-square—I found no such person lived there—I heard no more of him for a week afterwards—he was apprehended on the Wednesday afterwards at Woodford, in Essex—I found my mare in the stable of a man named Jordan, at Woodford, and the chaise had been sold to Broad, a coach maker at Woodford—the harness was pawned at Stratford—the chaise was worth 10 l.;, and the mare 10 l.;
Prisoner. Q. Did that chaise belong to you? A. No—I have a licence to let chaises, and mine being all out, I got it of Epps over the way—it was my own horse and harness.
HENRY JORDAN . I am a carrier from London to Woodford, and live at Woodford. On the 8th of Aug. I saw the prisoner at the back of the Castle there—I had no conversation with him then, but the next evening, the 9th, he brought the horse and chaise up to my place, to put the horse up for a few days, as he said he was going to stop in the country—the chaise was put into the cart-shed—on Saturday, the 10th, he came again, and had the mare and the chaise out—on the 11th he had them out again, with the harness—on the 12th be came in the morning, and told me he was short of cash, and was going to take the harness to pledge—he went to Stratford in my cart, returned in the evening, and told me he had pledged the harness for 2 l.;—next day he took the chaise out with a different horse, stating he was going to Epping, to see the cricket-match—he returned in the evening with the horse without the chaise, and a harness which he had hired—he told me he had sold the chaise for 3 l.; to a Mr. Smith, of Chingford—I said I thought that was a very low price—he said he only gave 3 l.; 5 s. for it, and if I thought proper to buy the horse, I could have it for 4 l.;—I thought it very low, and I dispatched a man to find Mr. Davis, to see if he could ascertain whether it was right—the man returned in the evening with Mr. Davis—I did not buy the horse.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not work that horse three or four days? A. I worked it once to Laytonstone, by your permission.
JOHN BROAD . I am a coach-maker, and live at Woodford. The prisoner came with a horse and chaise, and told me he wanted to sell the chaise—he asked 5 l.;—I said I did not want it myself, but I knew a gentleman who wanted an old chaise, which was Mr. Smith, a solicitor—Mr. Smith came the same evening—I called the prisoner—Mr. Smith said to him, "Will you take 3 l.;?"—he said he supposed he must, but it was too little—Mr. Smith asked him what he was—he said an auctioneer, and be could get more for it in a sale-room—he sold the chaise to Mr. Smith, who paid him 3 l.; for it, in my presence—this was on Monday, the 13th of Aug.
RICHARD TAYLOR . I am in the service of Mr. Jordan. The prisoner rode in a cart with me to Stratford—there was a set of chaise harness in the cart—I was going into Mr. Press', a pawnbroker, with another article, and the prisoner asked me to pledge the harness for him, which I did—I got 2 l.; for it—I gave him the money and duplicate—it was the same harness which he had brought with the horse and chaise to my master's.
WILLIAM JAMES LAWS . I produce the harness, pawned for 2 l.;, by Taylor.
JAMES WHITE ( police-sergeant K 13.) I took the prisoner at the back of the Castle, at Woodford, at a private house—I told him he was charged with stealing a horse and chaise—he refused to give his name and address—I found the mare at Jordan's stable, at Woodford, and the chaise at Broad's, the coachmaker's—I received the duplicate of the harness from Mr. Smith.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18440819-2214]
2214. THOMAS WALTER WILSON was indicted for stealing 1 chest, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1152 boxes of lucifer-matches, 2 l.; 12 s.; the goods of Samuel Elcock Molyneux, his master; and JOHN DYKE and SAMUEL SAVAGE , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL ELCOCK MOLYNEUX . I am an engineer and lucifer-match maker, in the City-road. Wilson was my clerk—it was his duty to enter the orders in the order-book, then in the delivery-book, and then send them out by Brook, the carman, who would have to take them—the delivery-book was always placed in Wilson's possession, to be sent out with the carman, where his goods were sent—there is no entry in the order-book kept by Wilson of any goods sold or delivered to Mr. Carter, of Croydon—there is an entry in the delivery-book—there is no corresponding entry without the name that could be applied to these goods—we have no customer of the name of Carter, and never had—these are the two chests of matches—supposing each chest to contain eight gross of match-boxes, their value would be 2 l.; 12 s.—I have never been paid for either of these chests of matches.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How do you know that they belong to you? A. By the chests, and the nature of the matches—lam away about eight months out of twelve—Wilson is not authorised to execute orders on his own account, or on mine, unless they are orders that I send up, or customers who are known to us—I was away when this charge was made—if a person were to come into the shop, and apply for goods to be sent, the prisoner would not have authority to send them, unless the customers were known, or he had consulted me or Mr. Riding—I do not know that the prisoner has made bad debts in sending out the goods—he might have made bad debts, but it might have been my orders—I have called on him to pay money when he has collected accounts and not accounted for them, but never for bad debts—there was not one of 6 l.; near Southampton—my mother is not liable for the debts of the business—she might have paid accounts for me in London—she has the charge of the money—I keep a ledger, and we post it from the order-book.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How Jong is it since Dyke left your employ? A. Last December.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Is there any entry of an order, on the 17th of July, to Carter, of Croydon? A. There is not, nor in any part of my books.
JOHN BROOK . I am carman and packer to the prosecutor. On the 17th of July I saw two chests of congreves directed by Wilson to Mr. Carter, of Croydon—one chest contained eight gross, and the other ten gross—they were directed to be taken to the Talbot-inn, Borough, and left there till called for—I can swear to this being one of the directions that Wilson put on the cart, and I believe the other to be the same—the day before I took them, Wilson told me he would give me a pot or 1 s. if I would get them out by ten or eleven—he told me not to tell the cooper anything about it—I took them to the Talbot in my master's van.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you think it was wrong what he told you, not to let the cooper know.? A. I had my doubts about it—I do not know any one named Carter—I had taken goods in that name before by Wilson's order—I had taken one to the Cross Keys, Saint John-street, for Carter—I entered it in my book, but it has been torn out by Wilson—he has the book after I come back—I found it out since we detected this—I was
first spoken to about it on the 17th of July, by Riding—the prosecutor was out of town at the time.
THOMAS RIDING . I am the prosecutor's superintendent. On the 17th of July I saw the chests in my master's van, directed to Carter, of Croydon, in Wilson's writing—I followed the van—I examined the carman's book which was in the care of Wilson, except when the carman had it—I went to the Talbot, and saw the two chests delivered into the yard—I sent for a policeman—I saw the directions after they were delivered into the yard—they were delivered at Mr. Moore's offices—I afterwards saw Savage at the police-station—he handed me this bill—it was Wilson's duty to enter in the order-book goods that were sent out.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was Wilson's situation inferior to yours in point of emolument? A. Yes—it was my duty to examine the delivery-book from time to time, but perhaps I have neglected it now and then—I do not know Carter, of Croydon—Wilson and I were good friends—it was his duty to attend to the books when Mr. Molyneux was absent—I was on the premises all day—if a customer came, it was Wilson's duty to send for me—I have looked through the books—there is no such name as Mr. Carter, of Scotland.
JOHN WESTMORLAND ( police-constable M 233.) I went to the Talbot-inn on the 17th of July—I saw Mr. Riding there, and he pointed out two chests to me—they were directed to Mr. Carter, of Croydon—I saw the prisoner Dyke come to Mr. Moore's office where the two chests were left—he looked at them, and removed them from Mr. Moore's office to Mr. Stanbury's office—Dyke then tore off the directions which were on the chests, and put them into his pocket—he went away—he came back within an hour, and spoke to Mr. Stanbury, who allowed one of his porters to take one chest away along with Dyke—I followed them to the prisoner Savage's, No, 17, Mint-street—the chest went in there, and I remained outside—I then saw Dyke and Savage come out together—I followed them to a spirit shop at the top of the Mint—I took Savage, and called Moy to take Dyke—I found 1 l.; 18 s. 3 d. on Dyke at the station.
JOHN MOY ( police-constable M 207.) 1 assisted Westmorland in taking Dyke—he asked what authority I could produce for taking him—I told him he could learn the charge at the station—he immediately ran away—I pursued, and took him—this money was found on him—I found in his pocket-book book these two directions, "Mr. Carter, Croydon, to be left at the Talbot-inn, Borough."
BENJAMIN STANBURG . I keep a booking-office in the Talbot-inn yard. I know the prisoner Dyke—he went to me by the name of Carter—I remember two chests being at my office on the 17th of July—Dyke called on me, and requested that one might be taken away with him, and the other left—he signed my book in the name of Carter as a receipt for the one chest.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long before had Dyke represented himself as Carter? A. He called once before for a chest that was left in the name of Carter.
WILSON — GUILTY . Aged 31.
DYKE — GUILTY . Aged 28.
Confined One Year.
SAVAGE — NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440819-2215]
2215. EMMA COOK was indicted for concealing the birth of her child.
WILLIAM SUTTON PHIPPS . I am secretary to the Lambeth Waterworks Company, in Blackfriars-road. The prisoner was my servant for ten weeks—she
came on the 23rd of March—I noticed that she was stouter than females generally are—on the 3rd of June I was up between six and seven o'clock in the morning, at work in my garden—I saw the prisoner in the kitchen, resting between the dresser and the table—she appeared very ill—I called my wife who came down, and we got a cab and sent the prisoner to her aunt's, in Albany-road—I went with her aunt that evening to the station, and the officer went with me to my garden—I gave him a spade, and saw him take the body of a child out of my garden.
ELIZABETH PHIPPS . I am the wife of William Sutton Phipps. I thought the prisoner was an unusual size, and told her I thought she appeared in the family way—she denied it, and said she thought she was dropsical-—on the morning of the 3rd of June my husband called me—I went into the kitchen, and saw the prisoner—she said she had got the spasms very bad—she went home—I took the sheets off her bed to give to the laundress, and from the appearance of them I thought she had given birth to a child—something I found in the water-closet confirmed my opinion.
CHARLOTTE LAWLESS . I am the prisoner's aunt. On the morning of the 3rd of June she was brought to my house in a cab —I had before that observed her person to be rather stout, but I thought it was dropsy—she said she was very unwell, and Mrs. Phipps had sent her home for a few days—I went to Mrs. Phipps, and when I got home I accused her of having given birth to a child—she said she had, and had buried it in the garden—she told me who was the father.
GEORGE EARL ( police-sergeant M 2.) I went to Mr. Phipps's, and turned up the mould in the garden—I found the body of a new-born child—it had not anything on it.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there much earth on it? A. No, not above an inch and a half or two inches.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Strongly recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Judgment Respited.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440819-2216]
2216. FRANCIS THOMAS SCOTT was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying John Samuel Read.
MR. O'BRIEN conducted the Prosecution,
JOHN KELLY . On the 15th of July I was in the Blackfriars-road—I saw the prisoner riding down the New-cut—I was about crossing over to the other side—I cried out, seeing a man crossing just at the moment that the prisoner was up to him—I also heard the prisoner cry out after me—he was riding at a very rapid rate indeed, galloping and urging on the horse, beating it on the side of its flank with his hand—I saw nothing in his hand—he had a spur on—he was quite near to the man when I hallooed out—the man was crossing from Rowland Hill's chapel—there was plenty of time for the prisoner to pull up—there was no traffic of carts at the time—he could have seen the man—the deceased had got about half-way across the road—I ran as fast as I could, not knowing which way he would turn, and by the time I got across he was up opposite me, or very near me—I saw him knocked down by the horse.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was there a lad with the old man? A. Yes—I did not see whether he ran away, I was rather confused at the time.
SUSANNAH MARY KNOX . On the 15th of July I saw the prisoner in the New-cut, coming across to Charlotte-street, riding at a very furious rate—I saw him urge on the horse with his right hand—he applied his spur, and likewise beat the horse with his right hand—I saw him some
time before I saw the deceased—the deceased was crossing from Rowland Hill's chapel, across Charlotte-street, when he was knocked down—I heard Kelly cry out three times—when I first heard Kelly cry out the deceased was not far from the horse's head—he had not time to get out of the way of it—I do not know which way the child who was with him escaped, but I think Kelly's hallooing made the child let go the father's hand.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the deceased's hat blow off? A. Yes, when he was close to the horse's shoulder—I was looking straight up the New Cut—the prisoner was coming towards me, and towards the deceased—he took his spur off afterwards.
JAMES THOMAS READ . I was with my father, John Samuel Read, on the 15th of July, crossing from Rowland Hill's chapel across Charlotte-street—the prisoner was just going to knock my father down when I first saw him—I was by Rowland Hill's chapel when I first heard Kelly cry out—I only heard him call out once—I did not hear the prisoner call out—I was behind my father when he was knocked down—I did not cross with my father—I stopped to look at some sheep which were going down Blackfriars-road.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear the prisoner halloo out? A. Yes, just as he was going to knock my father down—my father's hat blew off, and instead of turning after me he stooped to pick it up—he was in the road when his bat blew off—I was on the pavement—when he stooped to pick up his hat the horse jumped over him, and kicked out his hind legs, and struck him on the head.
COURT. Q. Were the lambs between you and your father in the road? A. No, they were just behind my father in the Blackfriar's-road—they had not come up to my father—they were going in the same direction—they were not against the prisoner—my father's side was towards the horse—it was a regular crossing.
FREDERICK LE GROS CLARK . I am assistant surgeon at St. Thomas's Hospital. On the 15th of July, Read was brought into the hospital perfectly insensible—the cause of death was extravasation of blood into and upon the surface of the brain—I inquired into the nature of the accident—the blow and concussion that he received were the cause of death—the appearances were such as were likely to have been produced by the accident described—there was a small scalp wound which was trivial, but there was extravasation of blood sufficient to cause death.
Cross-examined. Q. Suppose the horse to have cleared him by jumping, was the blow on the head likely to have been the effect of a kick from the horse's heel? A. Very likely.
MR. CLARKSON called
THOMAS ROBERT COWLING . I was driving my own omnibus this day from Kennington-gate to the Angel at Islington—I saw the prisoner on horseback—there are four roads meet at the spot described—I saw the deceased and the little boy about the centre of the road, in the carriage way, and the prisoner coming along out of Great Charlotte-street to go into Little Charlotte-street—he was in a condition to see the deceased before he called out—he called out twice—he pulled up sufficient to have cleared the deceased if he had not turned, but his hat blew off, and he stooped—the boy ran back again to the pavement—while the deceased was stooping the shoulder of the horse struck him in the side, which knocked him down—I did not see whether the horse kicked him or trod upon him—the prisoner was travelling a gentle canter, at the rate of seven or eight miles an hour—he certainly was not galloping furiously.
MR. O'BRIEN. Q. When you speak of the centre of the road do you
mean the centre of the crossing? A. Yes—there is a crossing, but the deceased was not on it—he was by the side of it, more remote from the prisoner—I was about twenty yards off when I heard the call out.
GUSTAVUS WILLIAM BLAND . I am a consulting surgeon. I am not at all acquainted with the prisoner or his friends—Cowling told me the prisoner was to be tried for manslaughter—I said perhaps it was important for him to have as many witnesses as he could, and as a matter of justice I had no objection to state what I knew—I was riding upon the box of Cowling's omnibus this day with the driver—we were going very slowly, and were very close to Great Charlotte-street—I saw the prisoner coming along on horseback, at the rate of seven or eight miles an hour—the horse lifted his feet very high, and made a great noise, something like what they call a butcher's trot—when about half way across Black friars-road the prisoner hallooed out—the deceased was passing along from Rowland Hill's chapel, as if going along Blackfriars-road towards the Surrey gate—he was not going along the crossing that is used there—he was further in Little Charlotte-street—the boy was with him—they made a movement to cross before the horse, after the prisoner had called out—I did not notice the deceased's hat fall off, but he seemed to make a halt and hesitation, and turned round—the little boy ran back, leaving him in the road, and it was then that he was struck by the horse's shoulder—the prisoner was not beating or spurring the horse at that time that I saw—he checked when he hallooed out—he might have stopped the horse entirely, but I considered at the time it was the deceased's hesitation, and turning round in the road which caused the coalition—I should say the prisoner pulled up his horse within six yards of the deceased from the place where he was struck—he turned back of his own accord, and rode back to the accident, and then the omnibus I was on drove on—I had no idea the coalition would have killed the deceased—he was not thrown forcibly on the ground, but made a rotary motion, and then fell—I do not think there was any kick, as from the position of the parties, if there had been, it would have been at the lower part of the man, not his head—I did not observe anything furious in the prisoner's driving—it was not a place which was likely to be dangerous—I consider he gave sufficient warning, that if there bad been more persons on the crossing they might have got out of the way.
MR. O'BRIEN. Q. You could only see him when he came on the road? A. Yes, I saw him about ten yards before that—whether he was spurring or whipping his horse previous to that, I do not know—I heard the prisoner halloo out twice at least—I did not hear any one halloo out previous to him—I did not hear persons cry out after the accident, "Stop him"—I was in a condition to have heard it—I will not say no one did cry out.
JOHN KELLY . I wish to correct this gentleman's statement; it was two men that ran from each side of the road that stopped the horse—it was at least sixty yards from the place. Witness. It is utterly false—there was not a person so situated that they could stop him—there was only one person went after him, and he did not reach him before he turned back.
WILLIAM LITTLE . I am a farrier, and live in King-street, Lambeth. I know the horse the prisoner was riding on that day—it is an old horse—I should say it was not capable of going at any extravagant rate—he would go at the rate of six or seven miles an hour—one of his fore legs is a stiff leg—he cannot bend it.
NOT GUILTY .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, THE 16 TH OF SEPTEMBER.
